LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAR NELLIE,

Amongst the dresses of a friend of mine is a grass lawn gown of open texture, made up over a batiste skirt, both bling in the natural, undyed tint. The small frills round the skirt prevent it from clinging in to the wearer's heels, as it might otherwise do. Each frill is bordered with narrow black velvet, and the scallops up the front or the bodice have a similar binding, opening over a vest of folded lace like that which forms the yoke. The latter is bounded on every side with bands of finely tucked muslin. Another lady I know has a white silk dress emb:eidered in black on bodice and skirt, and made up over frilis of the same, each bordered with a trimming of black lace in- serted.

My opinion is that large shady hats, plentifully trimmed with flowers, and bonnets are likely to be the fashion, as more countrified than toques. Feather are little used, and 80Me of the poke bonnets have no crown at all, leaving the coils of hair on the top of the head visible; others have curtains of lace falling over the brim all round, and these are tied with tulle or lace strings. But whatever be on hats, and no matter what the material of the dress, I must impress on you the fact to bear in mind the mike must be of studied simplicity, with nothing to break the lines of the figure.

I cannot help but notice the prevalence of open. necked dresses out of doors in the daytime. Crystal buttons arc being much employed on thin cloth and muslin dresses, and very pretty they look. A dress a friend had just come home from the dressmakers was in pink-flowered linon de soie, with a row of fine elear crystal buttons, the size of a sixpence, sloping from either shoulder, curving in at the waist and out again to the hem of the skirt. A pretty front of yellow guipure occupied the space between the rows of buttons, whilst the tight sleeves were slashed up the outside of the arm, fastening with the buttons and corded loops, leaving the arm visible. Another pretty toilette was being worn by her companion. It was made almost entirely of fringe, the small tight-fitting upper tunic being of lace, irom which hung a deep-shaped flounce of knotted and corded white silk fringe, slightly caught up on one side by a bunch of moss roses ; the plain lace bodice had a deep collar and revers of the fringe, and round the waist was a narrow waistband ot pink panne, with a knob of rosebuds at the side.

Many dressmakers are now engaged in making the last effert of what has been a remarlesbly brilliant dressy ceaeon, and you may expect to find that lovely dresses whl be forthcoming till the autumn fashione arrive. The majority of the frocks show great ..impiicity. MII3IIIIB and mous- echoes de sow, cle Je chine, lace, aud tatfatas i lead, and there s ae exquisite new material which resembles that structure resulting from spun glass. Foulard receivee is dee measure of attention. Haud-ombrold, red robes are being prepared to a great extent, and vary be.autiful they look. Oue I saw in pale blue mousseline scattered with sprays of forget-me-nota aud feathery white flowers, each spray seemingly tied together with a bow of appliqué lace :need ion. A promieent feature of travelling,, yachting and seaside dresses is the quantity of sietcnin lavished on the costumes, geeerally in straight lines, but sometimes in little curves or trefoils. In all the newest gowns the shape of the shoulders and of the •tops of tho arms l 3 accurately defined, the excep- tions being those landicem on which epaulettes or ridges of tucks or stitching interrupt the outline. Sleeves are as tight as ever. E?oow sleeves have come in with trio heat, but the long gloves that have to be worn with them are even hotter than length of sleeve. The bridesmaids at a great Wedding I was at solved the ditfieuity by wearing lace sleeves transparent irons shoulder to elbow, there frilled with biee, and gieves of only eight. button length, which Idt the pretty rounded elbows uncovered, and consequently cool in the crowded church. Probably the coolest gown of all i is one n thin homespun, or foule, lined with -muslin of a light make, and of a mushroom tint, like that of the fettle. A little white should be introduced for cooloes9 ot effect, as in bands of stitched linen, or batiste, round the skirt, and a white vest or chemisette should be in muslin, or striped gauze, one of the coolest of dress fabrics.

Your loving J NNNI,II.

THY:: MOST NUTRITIOUS.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

For instance when the Empress has visited Her Majrsty, the armouncetnt-nt of the daily drive has never been wordel " The Queen drove out accompanied by the Empress Eugeaie," but 1' The Queen and tte Empress Eugene drove out "the wording implying equality of rank,and station, as it was intended to do.

There is a pretty story of a visit to Paris of the ex- Empress, when heavily veiled, and in strict incognito, she entered the shop of a florist: A humble bouquetiere qu ckly ?cognised her, and offered her Majesty a bunch of violets (the emblem, as all the world knows of the Bonapartists) at the same time remarking, " Their fragrance never dies Madame." A similar comment might be made concerning that splendid chapter in the history of Scotland, which was oommeraerated the other day by the ihauguration at Inverness of the, Flare Macdonald statue. The story of this heroine's devotion to Prince Charles is too well known for reentiti ee here, but the best of it all is that Fora Maccleneld was by no means alone in her conspicuous loyalty. From April to September, Clinics was a wanderer in Scotland, with a reward of £30,000 offered for hie capture, yet he was not betrayed, although his secret was entrusted to several eundreds of peop,e, many of them poor. REVENGE OF VALERIE:

A ROMANCE OF BRITISH COLUMBIA

BT. HIIME,_ NISBET,

Author of "A Sweet Sinner," "Bail Up," "The Rebel Chief," "Kings of the Sea," "Kunting for Gold, "The Swarupers," eze.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Sir Lewis Eastlake, twenty-four years of age, is introduced to the reader, as he is "going the pace," at a London gambling club. There, in company with a -friend, Captain Eric St. Arnoul, he gets into an altercation, which results in a duel, at Cherbourg. His opponent is killed in this. He has a strong sentimental friendship with the Duchess of Valerie, at Genoa,a widow lady, in the prime of her beauty, who, in affection for him, backs the financier, who lends him money. Captain St. arnoul, his com- panion, who is a cousin of the Duchess, informs her that Sir Lewis had been injured in the duel, and in view of the other man's death, it is thought prudent he should be removed from Cherbourg. The Duchess hears that he is in charge of a Cherbourg doctor, who is an old friend, and his sister, who is a nurse. She fears that he may be arrested, and, arranges with the Captain for him to be taken by the nurse to her seat at Fataise, and she follows on afterwards. The Duchess finds, to her regret, on her arrival at Falaise, that the nurse "Hope" is young and good-looking, and a friend of Sir Lewis from childhood. After Hope has left he tells the Duchess that he and Hope had been engaged for years, and that before the, duel they Ncere married privately.

CHAPTER VII. IN DAWSON CITY.

It was nearly the end of February, in Dawson City, Yukon District, and the inhabitants were in a high state of jubilation. This was not on account of a new gold region being discovered in the Yukon Valley. Discoveries of this kind were too commonplace to raise the city to this pitch of excitement and joy. It was because an enterprising ex-cowboy 'speculator from Texas had succeeded in making the winter trail from Wrangel on the Stikine River, and so brought relief to the well-nigh starved out community. Bill Sykes was the cognomen of this ex-cowboy, when in society and on his dignity; linify Jerk, amongst his free and noble companions of the lariat and the revolver, because of the masterly style in which In could jerk or pitch his knife into a man twenty prds distant. "Jerk," his bosom friends called him for short.

He had been the special pride of the ranche for years past, always ready to head a playful expedi- tion against the Apaches, or in a raid on some of their peace-loving neighbours. Riding like demons through the streets of the nearest village, emptying their revolvers right and left as they rode along, with a fine disregard for life; wrecking drinking saloons, torturing tender-feet and doing the count- less' other free and chivalrous deeds which tile gallant American cowboys are so conceited about doing, and which make them such a respected and dearly-loved section of the glorious West. In pre- paring for such little outings Bill always provided himself with a lavish supply of sharp-pointed knives and never grudged giving them away, hence his picturesque sub-title. Although his companions were one and all proud of him and his high-spirited exuberance, Bill had found it necessary to shift his camp and seek pastures fresh after an epoch-making career down south.. At this time the Klondike fever had seized upon him. Promptly borrowing some gold-dust from a successful mining stranger, whom he chanced to meet on the trail as he was riding for his life, he started north. The marshal and his men found the corpse of the miner hours after- wards, with one of Bill's tell-tale knives in his back, but the gay cowboy was beyond capture by that time.

If the absence of "Jerk" was regretted down Texas way his timely arrival in Dawson City was hailed with the wildest delight. He had laid out that borrowed gold-dust in provisions Evid cattle, carried the cattle along as far as he could, and afterwards hired reindeer sledges and Indians, and brought his store intact up to the Yukon. . He was the hero of The hour, and likely to be one of the wealthiest 'Men in the city; besides, what he traded in was worth practically whatever he liked to ask for it. _.

had maae a rasnion tor nimseit in the shape and trimming of his furs and skins. The mounted police were the most uniform, but as for the others, they had all sorts of tags, tails and adornments hanging to their leggings and wide-sleeved coats. The only person present whom Madge was not intimately acquainted with was the new arrival, "Jerk," therefore, woman-like, she was inclined to devote her attentions exclusively to him. Forms and rough-hewn benches ranged along the massive plank wall. In the centre of the mud- baked floor stood a covered stove, which gave out a great heat for a few feet. Over the inner door hung a heavy bear-skin to keep out the keen air. There were two doors, with a small passage between, and both the inner and the outer doors were protected by these warm hangings of skin. There was no other ventilation to this room except the door, nor was any other required, for, as cus- tomers entered and swung aside the furs, they brought sufficient frigidity with them to freeze the beards of those standing nearest the exit. Windows were only used in summer in Dawson City. 'ln the winter they were hermetically sealed and shuttered. It was a long night of months, so that none expected windows to be unshuttered. Outside in the moonlight the dry icy wind was blowing a hurricane. That moon had been with them from its crescent stage until now at its waning. It would not quit the sky until it was blanked out. Dawson citizens had grown used to these extraor- dinary phenomena of Nature—the moon that circled round and round the snow-clad earth, through that long night, and the one stretched out day of sunlight. They became used to the deadly bite of the winter air, and almost used to the ferocious bitings of the tiger mosquitoes in summer. Bill Sykes as a good-looking and dashing fellow, after the stage villain and Buffalo Bill type, with long tresses and massive moustache. His eyes were small and black, his jaws firm and square, his figure tall, gaunt, but strong as if wrought from steel. In costume also he was the most picturesque figure in the room. Indeed, one glance was enough to mark him out as cowboy from top to heel, by anyone who had seen the ideal cowboy on exhibition. Madge had seen Buffalo Bill and his merry men in their gallant show, she had also read a great deal about the aristocratic parentage and heroism of the cowboy, and at once lumped to the conclusion that she had a disguised nobleman at her counter; a nobleman under a cloud, of course, for he would not be a cowboy unless he had been forced to abandon his title and estate; yet, despite all his early faults, one who had won the highest honours at Oxford and was quite able to correct any American professor of Greek or Latin, as all cowboys are described in romances to be. Filled with girlish admiration for this romantic and noble order of ,beings, she resolved to question this present specimen directly and bravely. Her chance came. He was regarding her with admiring looks when she suddenly started upon him, with a seductive smile. "You're a cowboy, I know, sir." "I was. But how can you tell, missy ? " "By instinct. I knew you were one the moment I set eyes on you." _ _ _ _

"Will, that is a fine instinct," he said, fixing his small but bold black eyes full on her. "And what else can you tell about me ? "

"That you are of blood royal—perhaps a prince. Eh ? "

I "Not exactly; no, not nearly so high up in the peerage, miss. "A. duke, then, or the heir-presumptive to a duke ? "

"Not quite that either," he replied, twirling his moustache.

The miners, driven-by cold and hunger into the city, were pretty well at the mercy of this Texan bravado. They had devoured all the tinned meat and salted pork; beans and flour were only to be had now by the very successful. They had whiskey, gin, and rum, or, at least, the raw spirits that Had been christened those names, at little more than summer prices. Tobacch had risen to six times its former rates. The Indians who hung about the settlement were still able to bring in some fish from the larger frozen rivers and creeks by keeping blow-holes open. But' this was not a dependable supply, and fish, without any other kitchen, even Klondike salmon, became nauseating food when taken constantly. Stomachs yearned with an unutterable yearning for a change of diet, as well as a greater supply of food. Then, when they took delirium tremens in Dawson City, they saw animated beef-steaks and agile Irish stews dangling in front of them and eluding their grasp, instead of snakes and varied- coloured imps. Women had their love visions and tender haunting memories, but those visions and memories were about former breakfasts, dinners, and suppers, no longer about men. They had come, both men and women, to seek for gold, and they had found it in such quantities that they could not have believed had they been told of it. Those lucky enough to possess claims were the leaders of society now, and did not mind paying whatever was asked, since their prospects were unlimited. _ _ _

"An earl, at least ? " "I might have been, had my father been one, but unfortunately he wasn't." "Then you are a lord, surely?" "Wall, no, I ain't." "Oh, say that you are at least a baronet or an honourable," cried the maiden in great distress. "I shall if it will ease your mind any, miss, but I doubt if it would be quite Gospel truth." "But you can talk Greek and Latin and all the other dead languages better than any professor? All cowboys can do that, surely ? " "Wall, I don't know that I ever did hear any cowboy do it. Yet we are all pretty fair at language sometimes, particularly when we are raged. She laughed at this reply, for she thought he was only teasing her, all the better to keep up his incognito. "You are very cute, but I know two things about you 'that you cannot deny." "Yes ? "

"That you come from England; and did some- thing before you left home. Also that Jerk' isn't your real name. There, now; what can you say to that ? " 'Yes, you are on the correct track at last, missy; I am originally from England, and I did something afore I left. Likewise, Jerk' isn't my baptismal or family name. Does that please you?" "Oh, yes. • Now, do tell me your real name, and I'll soon spot your title, for I've got an old Burke's Peerage' in my box, and I make a study of the nobility of England." He regarded her with a puzzled expression for a moment, then he answered gravely: "Wall, missy, my real bond-fide name is Sykes. Bill, or William, Sykes. Sykes with the 'to it." Madge regarded him angrily. Would he never stop chaffing her? At this moment Corporal Drum, of the mounted police, who had been standing close by listening to this conversation, chipped in and asked: "Sykes, with the y. Ah ; may I ask if you're one of the Blankshire Sykes's ? I knew one of that lot once."

Corporal Drum also had a weakness, like Madge, for the aristocracy of the home country. He was fond of tracing his own forefathers back to the Drums of Bumbledown. He had seen the Queen, and once visited a West End club; since then he had kept himself posted up on the old families. The ex-cowboy regarded his questioner luridly, while he played with his knife. He did not under- stand the drift of these queries. He began to suspect a trap, and was growing dangerous in con- sequence. Yet he replied sedately enough, all the while grasping his knife. "No, I ain't one of the Blankshire Sykes with the blank—blank y.' My family came from Wapping way, London, East. Any more questions,*pard ? "

Those who were nct so lucky, still had made small piles during the summer by working for wages, and were ready !o spend what they had earned, in order to be kept alive during the next three months: They could always get work,' winter or summer, at twenty dollars a day when they liked to work. _ _ _

Grub, not gold, had been the serious considera- tion of the past months, and now that this Texan adventurer had brought the one thing needful to the small city, he was feted, humoured and adored. They stood him drinks, even beyond his renowned capacity of thirst, and presented him with handfuls of nuggets. Women, old, middle-aged and young, smiled upon him as the Royal Duke of Edinburgh was smiled upon by the fair sex of Australia during his first visit to the Antipodes. It was enough to turn the head of any man, this open cupboard adoration, but it did not turn the level brains of Texas Jerk. He had come to Klon- dike to do a stroke of business, and he meant to do it. He acceptei the drinks, nuggets and sugary smiles with calm dignity, as his clue for undergoing the hardships of that winter journey in order to succour Dawson City, but he wasn't going to part with a single pound of frozen beef or flour without its full equivalent. He was a trader, not a soft- 'headed philanthropist. Having packed his stock in one of the empty stores placed at his disposal, he was now regaling himself at the bar of one of the most fashionable saloons. He had emptied an appalling number of glasses since his arrival, even in the eyes of the Dawson City soakers, but without apparent effect upon him. He was as calm, collected, and as pronounced in his nasal drawling accent as ever. Laconic in his replies to those who inquired about his a?ve.itures by the way, he could not be charged with bei ig a boaster. litr this reticence the men were disposed to like him. He was drinking at present. with a half-dozen of the mounted police who had dropped into the saloon. They were all fine, strong, hardy men, as, indeed, were the majority of the company present. Only the strong men had survived the winter, the weaklings had gone under. Madge Bowman, the daughter of the landlord, acted as bar-keeper. She was a Canadiari girl, who had been born and brought up amongst the severe snows of Upper Canada, and, therefore, thought but little of a temperature of sitxy-four degrees below zero outside. The saloon was well heated, at least sufficiently heated to feel almost too hot for those fur-clad loungers. Madge was a good-looking buxom girl, who was in the happy condition of being engaged to one of the most successful diggers. She, therefore, could spend her smiles and glances without being mis- understood by those who received them. Her lover, a young stalwart fellow of about twenty-six, was at present making himseif useful in washing glasses and obeying orders for fresh drinks. Madge measured out the quantity, while he handed the drinks round. There was nothing decorative about this saloon except, perhaps, rosy-cheeked Madge herself, and the picturesque costume of the visitors. Each one

CHAPTER VIII. BILL SYKES -GOES ON TIIE TRAIL.

It was an awkward and momentous crisis for the company to face, the pause that ensued after Jerk put that polite query to Corporal Drum. When men become soft-voiced and gently polite on Western ranches or Canadian diggings it is wisdom to keep a sharp eye on their hands. Not many present but knew; even Madge understood what kind of music was most likely about to begin, when Bill Sykes swung round so as to face this peerage-adoring policeman, and put both his hands negligently behind his back. The humorous smile that curled his thin lips deceived no one. Those mirthful sparkles that suddenly lit up, and seemed to make those Indian-like eyes grow larger and more devilish than before, might well have given the bravest of men serious' thought. Bill Wag' measuring his male questioner and try- ing to hold in his untamed passions, for he still remembered that he was on Canadian soil. On Texan ground he would not have waited one-quarter the time for the reply. "Any more questions about my family, pard ? " he repeated with a more ingratiating and oily sweetness than before. "I'll be most happy to oblige you, or any other boss present who may wish to play his little jest on this cove." Corporal Drum looked grieved that his friendly inquiry should be met in this spirit. He was a brave fellow who had faced death too often to know what fear was, and in spite of that little weakness of his, was greatly respected amongst his comrades and the camp in general. He knew that no one present would dare to accuse him of cowardice, whatever he did now. He had no desire to offend or quarrel with this inte- resting stranger. Therefore, he quietly placed both his hands on the bar counter, where they could be seen by Bill to be empty, and was just about to begin an elaborate explanation, which would have made matters ten times worse, when Madge came to the rescue.

"Don't be so touchy, boys. It was all my blame beginning such a foolish conversation." Bill gallantly turned to her and swept his fur cap off his head. "Don't mention it, my dear. A gal has privilege to ask what she blamed well wants from a man. But "—he turned once more to the corporal—"when a man, as I take you to be, pard, gets too curious, I like to satisfy him, if I can." "Look here, you blethering dunderskul!" shouted one of Drum's comrades, pushing the corporal on one side, and taking his place. What the mischief do you mean trotting out your old country dead- heads before a stranger, and disgracing your mates in this fashion? Excuse the corporal, chummy. Tie's one of the best we have for work, but he's a

THE FX ANENEP, FRIDAY, AUGUST 4, 1899

Pit soft--like maage, there, vn aristocrats, lie did not mean any harm." "Ah ; thought I was a bloated aristocrat, did he?" said Bill with a grin, recovering his good humour. "Wall, I aint ; nor am 1 vexea now that I know he wasn't trying to play possums. We do that to' tenderfeet' down Texas way, but I guess I've vaccinated long ago." Concord was once more restored, glasses were replenished, and the hours slipped past pleasantly. The next few days, if they could be called days, where so much darkness reigned, Bill Sykes did a profitable business, and at the end found himself richer than even he had calculated upon being when he first came in. He had supplied the city with what it lacked. He had also earned the gratitude of the citizens by bringing the first letters which they had received for the past six months; and although the postage had been more than ten times their weight in gold, still the news-famished diggers considered them dirt cheap at the price. Bill did not charge for delivering those precious epistles ; the receivers, however, insisted on plankng down their nuggets in exchange, and William per- mitted them to have their way. Through this gratuitous postal traffic he cleared enough to make him consider seriously upon becoming overland postman. A number of the letters, however, were still on his hands unclaimed. Some of the owners were absent on their distant claims, other owners lay under the snows, beyond all need for further earthly tidings. That is, their starved and frozen bodies lay under the heavy snow heaps, while their spirits may have known all that they cared to know about the writers. "Do you know anything about a party called Eastlake—Lewis Eastlake ? " Bill asked the in- spector of the district. "For I have several letters for him, and also messages from his friends." "Yes," replied the inspector. "He came here last summer, to examine several claims, that had been sold in London. These didn't seem to be much good, however, at this time, and looked panned-out; in fact, they looked pretty well cleared by the original owners before they vended them. But since then they have struck a deeper and a richer level, with splendid results. This Eastlake was greatly disappointed with what he saw, and went off with another man, by name Jack, Wade, to the Fort Norman direction to prospect, and as yet have given no report of themselves."

"Ah, I want to find Lewis Eastlake, for he has been recommended to me very highly, so I guess I'll take a turn up the same trail with some stores and seek for him. What sort is he ? " "As fine a fellow as ever came to the Yukon. Ile don't know much about digging, or be wouldn't have left this quarter so quickly; but his partner does, therefore I hope if they survive the cold to hear something good about them." Dawson City was not a beautiful town, composed as it was of rough slab huts. In the summer time it appeared at its worst, for then there were no snow and icicles to adorn the roof angles. The huts were bare, inside and out, being put up in haste and for shelter only. The time had not yet arrived for either men or women to dream about settling clown in Dawson City. They were only stopping long enough to make their pile, so that any cover served which would keep them warm. The utmost that was done in the matter of decora- tion was to stick up cuttings from the illustrated papers, or photographs of absent friends. Yet some of the unlucky ones might just as well have thought about settling down. Some had been long enough in this locality to be confirmed Sion- dikers. They had got so itsed to the fierce winter and the relentless mosquitoes that they could not have been happy anywhere else ; a more temperate climate now would make them uncomfortable, while some- thing would seem wanting if the buzz of the blood- suckers no longer sounded in their ears. When they had made up their minds long ago that the utmost which they could expect from Dame For- tune were grub stakes, and they had abandoned all idea of ever leaving the Yukon. When a digger has been ten or twenty years on the job without making his pile he generally accepts his destiny; the big prizes are not for his kind, and he becomes satisfied if he can make enough to carry him along year in and year out. There are, of course, many of this kind about every goldfield. They are thoroughly experienced miners, and know all about the practical part of their work, but they just manage to miss fortune, in spite of their skill, and through no fault of theirs. The hole they fix on may be only a few inches from the rich one, or they come too late or leave off too soon. It is their luck, they say, and no man can alter that.

In the winter time Dawson City is certainly more picturesque than in the warm weather. With three or four feet of snow covering all blemishes and resting on the roofs, it looks as if swaddled in well-bleached blankets. It is a dependable winter here; there is no uncomfortable thawing of that dry and powdery snow, so that it is not difficult to get about with snow-shoes or sledges. The rivers are, perhaps, the roughest and most rugged of the tracks, with the ice which has been forced upwards in hummocks. Overhead the star-filled sky bends, seldom completely dark since the Aurora. Borealis plays pretty constantly when the moon is not shin- ing. A grim kind of chilly dawning also marks off the twenty-four hours for a short interval. The population of Dawson City numbered at this time between six and seven thousand souls. Circle City had emptied its two thousand inhabitants into the more popular town' and all the lil ely ground for miles round had been taken up. Newcomers were compelled either to work for wages or else go out prospecting in wide districts. The Yukon,. however, was big enough to accom- . _

modate many times the number-of ad;;enturers that were already here, and as Circle City had been well stocked at the beginning of winter, starvation cases had been very few, and now that Bill Sykes had brought in a fresh supply they were not. likely to suffer great privation before the next season opened. Those who had spent all their gold-dust were trusted, as they pledged themselves to work off their debts. Those who possessed claims believed too much in them to part for any present offer. Bill Sykes had come, like most of the others, believing that there was gold to be got here, yet hardly swallowing all the tall rumours that had been floating about the States. But when he beheld the actual condition of things —the jam-pots, jars and bags of gold-dust and nuggets—they made him wild to think he had stayed away so long. There it was en evidence everywhere. In the dancing and drinking saloons he saw men and women pitching the stuff about with a reckless prodigality that put Texas and California com- pletely into the shade. On Sundays the respectable lot went to the sheds which served as churches and gladdened the hearts of the clergy by filling the plates with the precious ore. Men gambled away handfuls at a game, and permitted the girls they danced with to help themselves from their laden pockets. There was no closing-time here in the stores, restaurants, or hotels, and as yet no special licence required to sell liquors. Yet law and order were enforced and fewer outrages perpetrated than might have been expected under the circumstances. The reason for this, doubtless' was that., isolated as they were from the outer world, and forced into constant intercourse, each citizen knew all about his neighbours—what money they had and what prospects, with all the other secrets that are gene- rally kept hidden in civilisation. All nationalities were representea, and with the exception of an occasional dispute, the women and men were like one family. Bill took a good look round the camp, and after seeing some of the pan-washings on Bonanza Creek and Eldorado, he no longer had a single doubt left. The Yukon Valley held gold enough to swamp the world when it was properly exploited. He did a considerable flutter at the cards during his stay, and, as he was no novice at the games, he added materially to his store of nuggets, He tried also to get a share on some of the less-tested claims, offering all he was worth, but without success. He was told to go out and fird a claim for himself.

"There are hundreds of creeks lying waiting for inspection, as good and, perhaps, better than the Bonanza; go out and prospect for yourself." He was now firmly believed that this was so, therefore, after inquiring carefully and studying the official maps, he packed up three of his sledges, and, laying in a plentiful supply of ammunition, he set off towards the direction in which he had been told Lewis Eastlake and Jack Wade had disappeared. He was not aware of the title of the man he was af.ter. At Vancouver he had fallen in with an agent who had given him the letters and messages he was now carrying. The terms which this agent had offered him to undertake the task had been sufficiently handsome to interest him in the missing man.

Someone, to whom money was no object, had the preservation and welfare of this adventurer at heart. At Wranael Bill had been received by

DETECTIVE FERRET'S PLAN.

" If I ‘at-ra in search of a man of whom I knew notb, leg save tint he squinted, had a birthmark on his; right band, and limped ; and if I should see one who squinted only, that would be a poor clue, for thousandS of men squint. But if I saw a man who squinted and had the birthmark, the clue would be tea times stronger; and if tha limp were added I shou'd be practically cer. tam."

So said my friend, James W. Ferret, the famous detec- tive, to me last Thursday night when we got talking about cumulative probabilities. Anybody can see the force of that reasoning. We all rely on it, and use it constantly, without giving it an analysis. It is identi- cal in principle with the Bertillon system of identifica- tion, which is but a summary of trifles. It is when several small things are found in a group that they la& come prodigiously important as evidence. Here we may introduce a lady who says about her- self : "In the early part of last year I began to fed weak and ailing. I was tired, heavy, and weary, with' out bring able to assign a cause. I had no proper relish for my meats. and after eating suffered pain and discranfort in the stomach and chest. I got about ray bowie duties, so as not to neglect them, as you may says but nevev as I used to do and always in more or less pain.

" Mv uncle. Mr. Jaekson, who is a farmer, in thit neighbourhood, strongly urged me to take Mother Seigel's Syrup for the various bad symptoms that troubled me. To give me confidence in it, be said it bad cured him after the doctors had given him up. "On the strength ot what my uncle told me I sent to the Leigh Fiiendly Stores for a bottle of the syrup, and after using the medicine for a short time I could enjoy my meals, and all food agreed with me. I continued taking it, and was soon well and strong. Since then I have ileot in good health by taking a dose of Mother Seigers Syrup when I feel any of the slight ailmenta which will come upon us all even at the best of times: I want to make the following point for the good of any —especially women—who may be as ill as I was, or worre, not knowing how to name the com- plaint that afflicts them :—ln nine cases out of edema the real trouble is dyspepsia, or indigestion- Although indigestion is seated in the stomach it affect, every part of the body, as I know from experience; and this deceives many into thinking they have several disewes—pnhaps, consumption itse!r. It is only bY taking all these things together that we see that they are' parts of the same puzzle, and that indigestion is. the. true cause. You may use this letter of mine in any way yru like." (S;gned) (Mrs.) MARY Cnano,Platt-lane, Culcheth, near Warrington, January 10, 1899. "For many years," says another, "I was ill and wretched. I always felt weak, weary, and tired. I had a poor appetite, and what little food I ate gave me an awful pain at the chest and a sense of gnawing at the stomach, My breath was short, as in the case of people with asthma, and I w,,nt about worn and pressed down, hardly better than the poor things we see in the pictures of shipwrecks. I never got as much sleep as 1 ne(ded, and suffered both in mind and body from that, I was b.lious, too, and had the well-known signs of sluggish liver.

This, I say, was my condition for many years. No amount of medicine or doctoring did me any real good until, (leder the advice of one who had been cured by it, I began taking Mother Seigel's Syrup. That, no dlabt, is why Mother Seigel's Syrup cures snob a variety of complaints in curing ont ; they are all really cautoed by the main diease—l mean the dyspepsia." BRAG.—In the billiard room of one of the Fraser- burgh hotels the other night a game at "brag' took the place of a proposed game at draughts. A commercial traveller, who can play a decent game at the brod " and knows it, was,like Neal Malone, "blue moulded for want of a batin." He could Ploy any man in Fraserburg.he vowed, and became offensive with his brag. A quiet man in the corner oiaid-4. Ye micht do that, but ye'd lose." "I'll Play you for all that you are worth," said the coin- Marcia'. "Ye dinna ken fat I'm worth," said the quiet one," "but I'll play ye for a fiver." and he Produced a five pound note, and asked the corn- Merical to "cover that." The commercial hulked, lind began to mumble about draughts not being a elkillbling game. He had no objections, however, to toy the "quiet one" for a dram, "Na, na," said the tter, " doon wi' yet siller or stop yet gassin.' And t„he commercial traveller choke the latter alterna- tive. The point of the joke comes in with the ex- Planation that the " quiet one," as he himself put It, doesn't know the difference between a" king" and the "double six." "Were you not frightened about your money ?" I asked him. "Deli a bit. Man," he replied. "The marker tell't me there asna a board in the house."—" Fraserburgh Jclt ral d." FREEDMAN IN ANOTHER NEW ROLE. Mr. H. Freedman is strongly of the opinion that 100 much attention to the game of draughts is apt to do a player more harm than good. The figurPs thus (1145) indicate that a move is made from and to the squares so numbered. Black has the opening move and the pairs of figures apply alternately to Black and White to the close.

AN EXPENDITURE OF £6OB Al' LONGFORD.

It was resolved at a meeting of the above comrnitt.e that the tender of Mr. Thomas Davies to erec7, a new manure store at Longford D.Vit, at £6OB, be accepted. Councillor MONKS, in moving the coofirmatien of the minutes, referred to the resolution, to spend £638 upon a manure etere at Loagford, the reason being, he said, that the re-organization which bad been carried on for the last four or five years had rather crippled the stor- age accommodation.. yhose members of the Ceuncil who remembered what took place SOlll9 years ago would 'mow that the last store building was to accommodate 400 tons of dry manure. The quantity of material they had now to I &al with had increased to such an extent that it was necessary to provide at the present time room for 800 tons, and Inc:king to the development of the town dui- ing the next few years, it only seemed reasonable to provide for a minimum quantity of 1,000 tons. Thesum of £6OB would provide such a store, and he thought that would be the last considerable sum of money they would come to the Council tor —(hear, hcarl—as far as Long- ford was concerned, unless it was intended to either re- build the place or to adopt some system different en' irely from that which was in operation at the pre- sent time. Thl present store was utilized by two new dryers,. One of them had Wen worked in Manchester and one or two other towns and had elven every satis- faction. They themselves were very well satisfied with the dryer that had been working for some time, and the one started a few weeks ago. The work proposed was not particularly presdng,but still it was desirable to take it in hand in view of the present limited accommodation. This resolution wou'd be passed subject to the approval of the L:cal Government Board. They had in the past few years done certain work without obtaining the sanction of the Local Government Board, and they had managed to engineer it successfully through the House of COMMONS, and the accounts would be passed now without any necessity for securing the approval of the Local Government Board. He did not want to start, and the committee did not want to start again in that irregular way—(hear, bear)--and that was why he sug- gested that this should be sanctioned subject to the approval of the Local Government Board. As it would be necessary to have an inquiry in connection with the proposed electric lighting works, this inquiry could be held at the same time, and being such a small matter there would be no difficulty, In thought, in getting the sanction of the Board to the proposal. ELECTRIC LIGHTING. THE SCHEAE EXPLAINED AND TENDERS ACCEPTED.

I Councillor MONKS brought for ward a resolution:—"To consider, and if thought advisable, to accept tenders for the construction of the new destructor premises and plant, and the new electric lighting premises and plant, and other works ire connection there with." In doing so he said he had bad several important propositions to submit to the Council in previous years, but to is was the most important of the lot, and one that he felt a little anxious abut because it was so full of techni- calities and it was possible some misapprehension might arise for want of requisite knowledge on the part of some of the memb3rs. He took it for grantei te start with teat the Council had decided to have an electric lighting station of some sort. The fact that the Parliamentary Order was applied for and granted, made it necessary for them to taks some step within two years from that time. otherwise private I traders could apply to the Local Government Board for similar authority, and they (the Corporation) would have to put in an appearance and apply for an extension of time. Recent experiences had proved that electric light was just as important to the inhabitants as the gas and water undertakings. The Electric Lighting Committee was only appointed about six months age, and they had had that time to consider this expenditure of £51,000. The committee consistod of ten menabers, six of whom were members of the Sanitary Works Committee, and only four were, practically, new to the subject. For three years the Sanitary Works Committee have had the matter under consideration se that the proposition did not come before them as merely the product of six months' thought. That was a point which, he thought, might weigh with some members of the Council. By the aid of a plan upon the walls of the Council Chamber Councillor Monks proceeded to outline the proposals em- bodied in the electric lighting scheme. On the extreme left was the entrance to the unoccupied field where it was proposed to build the works. The entrance would be from Parr-street, and the roadway went along to the right, giving access to the Electric Lighting Station. Carts would bring the ashes from the houses, and tip them on the platform, which served for the men to convey the ashes to the destructors working in connection with the boilers. There were two boilers, so that they obtained steam from the destruction of this waste product. Between these two boilers and on either side were three other hand-fire boilers. The whole block was called the boiler house, and, to exclude all dust, was cut off from the rest of the station. Then there came the dynamos, then tee switch- board, and then there were the accumulators or storage batteries, which were necessary to provide against any breakdown of the machinery. There would also be an office, workshop, air-compressing room, and testing room. A chimney would be necessary for the whole of the boilers, and between the boilers and chim- ney it was proposed to erect a Green's Economiser, to economise steam asjar as possible. There was a space left for further extensions, in the shape of two more boilers,when they became necessary. They could extend in two ways. First by taking out the small dynamos and putting in larger ones, or by extending the premises. The premises had been designed of sufficient capacity to admit of extensions for the next tea or twentyyears. Coming back to the entrance they would see a road branching off to the left and giving access to the portion under the control of the Sanitary Works Committee. It was intended to build stables for the horses now stabled at the Town Hall, loose boxes, store, mess room for the men, and an office. On the other side of the roadway would be a cart-shed, a smithy, and cooper's shep. This completed Ceuncillor Monks's bare outline of what was proposed to be done with the money. The total area of land belonging to the Corporation was about five acres, of which they only proposed to take iinrnedietely, two acres. With regard to the scheme itself the Council had had Mr. Preece's report for some months and he supposed the members were familiar with it. They expected to save at least £2OO a year by carting the refuse to Howley instead of Longford, Alderman SUTTON asked if the tenders proposed to be accepted were the lowest. Councillor MONKS replied that they were not the lowest in every case. The committee had little knowledge or experience of its own and they felt bound to take Mr. Preece's opinion with regard to the allotting of these contracts. Mr. Preece had selected those firms which he knew would do the work properly and provide an elec- tric lighting station second to none in the kingdom. lie supposed the main question before the minds of his hearers, was, would the scheme pay? On that point they were in a difficulty them- selves. The Councillors would readily under- stand that the committee bad no experience but that of other towns, where Ot0)." the conditions were not quite the same as in Warringten. If they looked at the statistics in regard to other towns they would find in the great majority of cases that work had been carried on from the commencement without any loss, and without the ratepayers being called upon to meet any deficit. The work had, generally, been carried on at a profit, but ,in a few cases it had taken one or two years to achieve such a satisfactory result. Tne Committee felt that there was every pros- pect in Warrington that the work could be carried out without any serious loss. In the second year. at any rate, they &email be able to show a profit, and he had no hesitation in recommending the proposals to the Council, for their adoption, subject to the approval et the Local Gevernment Board. With regard to Mr. Preece. added Councillor Monks, he knew nothing about him when he was recommended and felt a little acxisty on the score of his youth. He consequently made enquiries in different parts of the country and was glad to be able to say that every reply he re- ceived was satisfactory. He was aesured on all hands that the Cerporation could very safely place themselves in Mr. Preece's hands. He believed Mr. Preece bad had the assistance of his f sther in connection with this work, and Mr. Preece, sen., was considered the leading I electrician in this country, so that they had had the best advice possible. It was proposed to proceed as soon as possible, and if the work could be put in hand at once there was no reason whatever why they should not have everything ready to light the town with electricity in the winter of 1,900, and in two years show a setisfao- tory return for the investment of the money. There was another point he might mention. If the Corpora- tion could eee its way to go into the tramway scheme it I would assist them:immensely. The electric lighting station would only be kept taiey in the winter for tiehtine one-

Ths DEPUTY MAYOR secendel the proposition, and to marked tsat he had no fear of the electric light interfering with the consumption of gas. In other towns the consumption of gas has increased with the increased demand for electric tight. Alderman Simon expressed indebtedness to Coun- cillor Monks for his lucid explanation of the scheme for electrio light. He also urged Ih3 urgency of proceeding with the scheme in order that no one &se could obtain powers for an electric light supply in the town. In his own mind he had not the slightest doubt that the scheme would be a Success. Councillor Dr. Caererera, said he did claim, on this sub- ject, to have some slight knowledge (" Really," and Laughter). There were not more than two or three of them who knew the difference between electricity and ("whiskey," laughter.) horses, added the speaker. There were points on the schedule before them that he did not understand. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Dynamos he could see, and boilers he could only see note "A." He thought it was a niece of downright impertinence to ask the Council for £50,000 and only 17 members present. (Laughter.) Couocillor Grilles said he thought that there had been enough delay. He tendered his congratulations to Councillor Monks and the committee for the fine and comprehensive scheme brought forward. He was con- fident of its Success. Councillor SHAW GREEN said he supported the com- mittee in this undertaking, but mentioned the fact that there was no reference to the land upon which the pre- mises had to be built. Although they were at present the owners of the land it was not paid for, and it would have to be brought in and dealt with by the Inspector when be held the inquiry. It was also in addition to all the expenditure sanctioned by the Act of Parliament they had just obtained. Councillor Monks had shown them reasonable prospects of the electric lighting scheme becoming a source of revenue in aid of the rates. He trusted the forecast would be true, and they would look upon Councillor Monks as one of their benefac- tors. Alderman ROBERTS said he would like to say, as a mere business man without special knowledge of the technicalities of electric lighting, that he thought they were going on safe and sure lines in adopting the pro- posals so ably put forward by Councillor Monks. He thought the Council might have every confidence in the fact that in the chairman and dep ite-chairrnan of the committee they had two men who knew something about electric lighting and mechanics generally. They had gone, as commonsense people should, to the man whom they considered one of the ablest men in this particular line of business in the country. Mr. Preece had advised them all through, and they had every con- fidence in his judgment and experience. As to the pro- bable results of the scheme they had had to go to places, where the light was in operation and by the results there they had concluded that if the experiment was pro- perly arid suitably managed,hnd the people of Warring- ton appreciated the fact that they might have light in their dwelling-houses and places of business it was bound to be a success. They had learned by the experience of others that to start on too small a scale would be a gigantic blunder. In every instance it was a fact that towns had had to expend considerable additional money to cope with increasing demands. The plans before the Council that nigat had taken advantage of such experi- ences, and the scheme provided for very considerable extension. He was glad the urgent necessity of tram- ways had been mentioned. He did not know a town in England of the size and importance of Warrington that was so absolutely behindhand in means of conveyance and accommodation as Warrington. Two of the main roads would shortly be permanently paved, and it was advisable that preparations should be made for elec- tricity and tram-lines before the pavement was put down. He supported the proposals most cordially in full confidence that Warrington would avail itself of the opportunities afforded. THE CONTRACTS. BOWLING.

LANCASHIRE v. YORKSHIRE.

The return engagement in the ai,nua.l meeting be- tween the above counties wam hill over the green ad- jacent t) the Trafford Hotel, Chester-rod, Manchester, on Saturday. The match opened at ChapAtown, near Leeds, on Saturday. 17,tifJune' when the homeside gainei a victory by 46 points. The Yorkshire team arrived in Manchester early on Saturday, and were per. mitted an hour's practice prior to play being commenced. During the earlir porticn of the engagc. ment the home team found their hands full in en- deavouring to prevent Yorkihire from extending the majority they brought with them from Chapeltowr., which at the close of the sixth game had risen from 46 to 60 points. Towards the middle of the encounter this had been reduced to 50, leaving Yorkshire in a better position than when they arrived on the green. The heavy win made by the Lincashite champion acted All a war cry to those who st,ll had to eeter toe lists, aud a run of successes resulted. Gradually the adverse balance was reduced, and then extinguished. After a brief ' break in the Lancashire su cenes, during which York- shire recovered 14 shots, winning games went on until the teams bad played out, when iv was found the home side had scored a victory by 32 on the aggregate. • THE EXAM EN E.U.

AUGUST 4th 1899.

IT will take time to as- The Church and the Judgment. certain what has been the effect of the Lambeth judgment, or whatever it may ba more accurately„ BtYled, upon the Church and people. The cieci- ion as to incense and lights is excellent on paper. it is only morally binding upon anybody, and

Where incense and -lights have been habitually nsed, with the full assent of the people worshipping at a given place, who may or may not be all Darishioners, it is not easy to see why any oh ange should be made. A strong clergyman may ask himself why he should obey what is not a legal de- eision, and why he should not persist in his con- duct until someone takes him to task. This atti- tude, however, would be quite inconsistent with the views held by High Churchmen, who prefer an ecclesiastical to a lay tribunal, and who have inti- izated their desire to follow the decisions of their ecclesiastical superiors. Still it may be said that the two Archbishops did not constitute a legal and an ecclesiastical court. Probably it may be afte time before any general movement is visible Leaders count for something in these matters, and the leaders of the entire High Church movement, and of the English Church 'Union in particular, have yet to pronounce their opinions. Doubtless they are in a difficult tactical position. They have ciamo•ired for an ecclesiastical pronouncement, 1111c1 here it is. They have confessed a readiness to obey their Metropolitans. On the other hand, it raay be said that these decisions savour more of expediency •than of principle, and that they amount to a concession to the Protestant party, the Erastians, in the interests of peace and tem- Porary contentment. It looks as if Vlq reservation of the Sacrament might be also condemned. What then, is the vary High Church party to do? Is 4t to temporise once more, and to openly allhinit, whilst seeking for the development of Priestliness in other directions? The Protestant Party seems ready to take the Lambeth ,statement as if it were valid and substantial, mak- ing it the basis of further agitation against Ritua- lism. The demand now is that the Bishops shall enforce the law, meaning thereby whatever is not either enjoined or permitted in the Prayer-book. KENSIT'S letter in yesterday's "Times "shews that he takes the Lambeth statement as a full justification for the past and an encouragement tor the future. "We are quite calm," he writes, ." but fully determined that the Romanising prac- tices so log indulged in shall cease, and in this We carry with us the great mass of the Protestant 'country." "in over three hundred constituencies We have electoral rolls pledging voters to put their Protestantism as a front plank in the coming flreneral Election." But Disestablishment is neither advocated nor believed in. What will such voters ,(1° When they have to choose between a Disestab- 4iahmentarian and a Ritualist or a nonErastian ? Thatis a case which will constantly occur. The • aitUation is thus interesting and complicated.

HALF the pleasure of life is Adventures to the said to consist of the unusual, Adventurous. the extra, the adventurous. Ihe hard-worked professional man risks his life in 41Pine climbing, or he tries his hand at boating, Yachting, driving, cycle touring. The city folk, W4O never have the chance of gaining much ex- Perience in out-of-the-way occupations, get up rocks and are unable to get down again, exhaust themselves in walking tours, or run all the risks inseparable from travelling in a country whose language they cannot speak, and whose manners :lOU customs they do not understand. The result- Lng adventures are manifold. They are supposed tot bo a part of the pleasure of holiday-making. 'f nothing unusual happened, what must be the (ivantage of sight-seeing, of foreign travel, °r of visiting unknown places? Probe- -1311/ the English people are as well to the front in these matters as any other people in the world. They like adventure for its own sake, 1-14 not for the sake of any knowledge acquired .t(mn it, or any gain in other respects. Accord- ingly there are more Englishmen in difficulties road during the holiday season than any other ecnntrymen. These difficulties involve detention, 4..oEnetimes imprisonment and fines, troubles with 'he railway and customs officials, enforced resi- tlenee in strange places until more money is ob- :ined from hcme, appeals to the British Consul ; the Ambassador, and endless other shifts, acci- °tits, annoyances, and perils. It will be readily tlciraitted by the Germans and the French that 'e37 have more troubles with British tourists than th any.other. They have an innate faculty for v,°itg the wrong thing abroad, saying the 111.°11g thing to an officious person, and mis- 114er5tan3ing the simplest instructions. A f!ve of adventure is the stimulating passion, and tut' the next two months or so it will be gratified i 4 he full. Even when in their own land, the b,°O'lish, Scotch, and Irish visitors will seek the t,4raordinary in some way or other. The odd 04,1,ngs that tourists do are unaccountable on any I:4er theory. They seem to be insane things to 8t other ptople, and when foreigners most 0 .toshmen abroad, and freely express their atl3inion about them to their own countrymen,they frequently heard summing up the case by say- -117 • "They are mad !" In a way, this is true. the World bears witness to our mad exploits, and i4t colonies we have acquired, developed,and made the independent communities, are a testimony to dness of the few for the gain of the many. 40543 islands were never intended to coop up the 4,ietgies of a people in whom so many strains of venturous blood have combined. Scratch the

===== 0002601_18990804_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
, _

Briton, and you find the Viking. One result of this adventurous, forth-going spirit is that 'our holiday accidents are more frequent and numerous than occur in any other country of the world. Just now we are realising the fact, and insisting upon all sorts of precautions, wise enough in them- selves. But the love of adventure, how can that be curbed?

Colonel Elsdale's paper in the "Nineteenth Cen- tury," entitled "Why are our brains deteriorat ing ?" begs the question. We fancy he will not find many people to agree with him in his initial premiss. He argues that becadic there is such a tendency among educationists:nowadays to develop the receptive faculties, therefore the cre,itive faculties, even if awaiting the ripening process, must be neglected, and in the end must tend to decrease in power throughout mankind generally. Disuse, of course, produces impotence, but the dis- tinction between the receptive and creative parts of the brain is not clearly enough marked to make the analogy a safe one. And where are the actual signs of brain deterioration F We have invented the X rays, discovered argon, the cinematograph, the application of electricity, and a wealth of con- trivances useful to mankind. In literature, just now, it must be confessed that creative power has not ascended to notableheights. Bat we are only passing through the usual cycle of eclipse—a cycle which has regularly appeared and disappeared since the golden Elizabethan age. This is true of the arts generally, though we fancy that future generations will not think the eclipse so complete and the darkness so dense as many of us at present affect to believe.

There was a good deal that was ridiculous in the resolution brought before the Wesleyan Conference by the Rev. T. Champness affirming that no Christian man should be a manufacturer or seller of intoxicating liquor, and, fortunately, the Con- ference refused to accept so sweeping a declara- tion. Had it done so, Wesleyans would, not un- justly, have been looked upon as a singularly bigoted body of men. Some of us may perhaps see some inconsistency in a man who professes Christianity and yet reaps a rich harvest from the sale of intoxicating liquor, but to say that no Christian man should be either a maker or seller of it would be exceeding the bounds of reason and common sense. There is a legitimate demand for alcoholic drink—as well as an illegitimate one—and surely the Rev. T. Champness does not require that sinners only shall be entitled to supply that demand. If h does, we should like to know how the acknowledge° or proven sinners are to be procured. .Such resolu- tions as this do much more harm than good. They tend to bring the cause of temperance and tem- perance workers into disrespect and ridicule, and at the same time alienate people whose sympathies may be on the side of temperance, but who will have nothing to do with teetotalism of such a rabid form. No greater blunder can be made that to push the crusade against strong drink to such an extreme, and advocates of these drastic measures only defeat their own ends by introducing -them. Teetotalism never will be- come universal,and temperance itself must rely for its general acceptance on a judicious education and elevation of the people—or in other and familiar words, " persuasion rather than force."

===== 0002601_18990804_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
_RUNCORN NOTES.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.]

The Fishing Club's match on Sa-turday was a delightful exhibition—and if those ardent anglers depended upon the product of the line for their dinner—why, like the sailor, they would have to be content with taking in a" reef" and with imagining the gusto with which they masticate' their " catch." The first prize was won with 2 ounces 1 drachum and the second with 2 ounces of fisbor rather eels. Nothing else would bite.

An object lesson was afforded those present at the meeting of the Runcorn Co-operative Society on Tuesday night, and the wonder is,that shareholders allow their chairman to be "bamboozled "in the manner he was, and tormented to death with use- less question& I should think a certain gentleman from "across the streak "asked about 50 questions concerning the balance theet—the nett result of his questions however was that not a single figure on the balance sheet was affected. Clearly the part of obstructionist was his. I would suggest that Mr. Fitzpatrick,—if he desires to serve the interests of the Co-operative Society bests—would exercise his privilege as a shareholder of inspecting the books himself and when he finds anything of real moment ventilate it. The discussions he gave rise to on Tuesday were to say the least, frivolous and un- called for.

Mr. T. Watson also diseovered a mare's nest—he had discovered that the committee had in its inner workings a " clique Naturally the inference was that this "clique" was militating against the inter- ests of the society. Re was asked in the face of the meeting to name his " clique " but did not. Still he adhered to the statement that one existed. Individually the other eleven members denied that they were party to any"clique"—and as Mr. Scragg put it, they had their characters to maintain, and did not like such an unsubstantiated statement floating about. Thus it would appear that Mr. Wat- son considers an opposition of 11 to one a "clique " and, that, simply because some of hi a pet theories concerning the ad vantage of a "week to week " chairman have been opposed.

Where do we stand? The inestion has been dis- cussed and the matter decided,so to speak,but with such reservation and circumspection that we just stand where we were. The park—the play grJund —the recreation ground—call it what you will, looks as if it going to be hampered:as much as possible. The bogie of expense is raised; though it is admitted that such an acquisition for £4OOO ie not to be picked up every thy. My advice to the council would be—go ahead with it. "he who hesi- tates is lost " and the same thing applie3 to coun- cils. Ido not like the passing ot the resolution with the" hedging "it has; and would suggest that a book and the plan of the scheme be laid open in the Town Hall for the ratepayers to go and put their signatures either in favour or against its immediate acquisition. And if the majority are for it, why "let 'em 'ave it."

I learn that it is the intention of the promoters of the Park Scheme also to provide baths for the town, and that too out of what they are able to save from the £4,000 which was tte figare esti- mated as the price of the Heath and Comtuop and the Hill. The angwested site of the bathe is the overflow "pit" near the waterworks, which, for a little cost, could be easily converted into an open- air bath.

From the line of argument taken up at the Coun- cil it would appear that an idea is prevalent that the Council will be allowed to borrow money piecz- meal to carry out a scheme piecemeal, Yet not- withstanding what the law clerk state l they might legally be able to do, they will be morally bound,if :they borrow that money at all, to US3 it for the purpose represented. Now I would a.,k where is the economy in proaristinatioe and small sums ?—We ,all know that business is beat transacted with the successful manipulation of large sums borrowed advantageously, and courage- ously applied; and it is rather on these lines the Council must proceed than the other. No fault is found with the scheme—it is admittedly AU all- round good one—and as it was to be carried out with a view to rp-,renchtnent where neces4ary, it would hare COM 4 under rather than over the esti- mate. I should like to sea the Council. or at least some of its members,take their stand by the scheme, and propose its definite acceptanca or rejection.

In connection with the scholarship' scheme of the Runcorn Institute Day Schools the Runcorn Co-operative Society have given three scholarships of the Yalu() of .t 1 7s 01 each. Dr. AfcLennan and Mr. P. Beck have eich promised a guinea a year for two years, which is very gratifying.

BATHING 'FATALITY AT WIDNES.

Roberton Anderson, aged 12, son of Robert and Mary Anderson, 10, Pleasant-street,was drowned at 4-15 p m on Sunday, in the Mersey, opposite Sullivan's works, Widnes. The deceased, in company with three other ;ads about the same age, went to bathe in the river. Tney waded and swam across to a sandbank, the tide o.ing out, and for a time amused themselves there. Wnile thus engaged the tide commenced to flow in. See- ing the predicament in which they were placed,the b took hold of each other's hand with the intention of a:listing one another. After going into the water a few yards they separated, and each endeavoured to swim ashore. Joseph Burstfield, 67, Jamee-,treet, noticing the danger the lads were in, went to thir help and rescued Bernard Donoghue. A foreman of Pilkirwton's works, named James Wilkinson, 20, Crois-s' re. t, helped to get the lad out. Robert Anderaon and 0 rren Rice were not seen again Wive, but James Fallen, the foul th boy, managed to swim ashore. The news of the disaster soon spread,and in a short time sever al strong swimmers were ready te go in search of the missing boys. Owing t) the force with which the current was running it was d,emed advisable to postpone operations for altime. Eventually some of the swimmers entered the water but were unable to find the bodies. The body of A.nderson wes recovered at 4-30 a.m. o a kfenday, abut 300 yards higher up the river. TA,.'s father of Anderson is a labourer, but at present is in the workheuae, the mother living at 10. Pleasaut-otreet, but in very poor cinum- stances.

THE ROLLING SHIP AT RUNCORN.

There is now lying at the Widnes Lock, Runcorn, the " Ernest Bazin "a rolling shin, the invention of a French engineer. The vessel is on its way to Manches- ter for exhibition. The object of the inventor was to miske his vessel roll over the waters without having to overcome the resistance of the water. For years the vessel has been the subject of great interest to engineers and the general public. Many persons availed them- selves of the opportunity of seeing the unique craft.

THE PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY AND THE TREATMENT OF SMALL PDX.

The Health Committee reported : "Dr. Crocker, the Medical Officer of Heilth to the Manchester Port Sani• tary Authority, had intimated that arrangements had been made with the Liverpool Port Sanitary Authority to remove and treat cases of Small Pox found on vessels bound to any part of the Port of Manchester, at a charge of four guineas per week per 'case," and recom- mended that the costs incurred by the Manchester Authority at the above-named rate be refunded in res- pect of Small Pox cases found on ward vessels bound for Runcorn.

RUNCORN WATER SUPPLY.

The following' report of the bacteriological analysis of Runcorn water, as received from the School of Patho- logy, Liverpool University College, was submitted "The sample of water received on the 21st inst., con- tained numerous colonies of bacteria when cultivated on gelatine and a few on agar, they were all ordinary water bacteria non-pathogenic. The water is of average pur- ity.—Yours faithfully, CHMILES A. HILL, M. 8., Assis- tant Bacteriologist." Mr. James Pritchard proposed the adoption of the Water Committees;minute s. Mr. Once secottded, and the motion was carried unanimously. Mr. Handley asked that a definition of" non- pathogenic" be given for the public benefit. Mr. Wilding explained they' were healthy bacteria, and, indeed, water would be unwh3lesome if they were not present.

Handley submitted the report of the Parlia- mentary and General Purposes Committee.which recom- mended the adoption of the plan prepared by the Sur- veyor for the improvement and laving out of the Common at an estimated cost of £4,000 be approved, and that application be made to the Local Government Board for their sanction to the borrowing of the afore- said sum of £4,000 for a period of 50 years, and doing so gave a detailed description of the proposed ptrk and recreation ground. Bisbrowa considered he was on the unpopular side of the question, as he was opposed to it, on the ground that the ratepayers could not stand the strain of any increase on the rates. It was unwise to incur any additional expense, which by keeping up the rate of the town to 3s 31 vu'uld stave off the settlement of any new industry. He had not the remotest ides, at the incep• tion of the scheme that anything like £4,000 would be required. Be considered it all very fine for those gentlemen who could afford to pay it, but there were other propsrty owners in the town who could not survive the additionil burden- He proposed that the matter be referred hick to the Health Commkttee.

Mr Grorge Hewitt seconded Mr. Bi=hrownis amend- ment. He asked was it correct that £2,000 was esti- mated for readmakinv, and £9OO for drainage? Mr. Lightburn contended they were compelled to make the roads, as part of the agreement by which they olatained po:session of the common. With regard to the £4,000, he took it that the r2so!ution they were asked to pats did not bind them to spend that amount all at once. They would simply have to spend that which they were compelled to do on the roads,and could deal with the other as.ocoasion gave rise. The discussion which followed turned on this point, and on the understanding that the Couozil were not bound to carry out the scheme as set out, at once, but could take up the loans as required, the minutes were adopted, with Mr. Bisbrown and Mr. Hewitt dissenting.

COMMITTEE'S REPORT.

The committee reported as foll)ws ro prefent you with the abstract of accounts, for the half-year ending June 30th, 1899, affords us great pleasare. A rapid glance at the details therein contained will convince you of the great strides eale ch the society is constantly making, as well as ef its great importance and widening influence. In the period under cerisideration we have reelised a total turnover of £55.481 12s 7d, which repre- sents the largest trade done with th •Society in one half- year since its establishment, some 37 Years ago. The takings show an increase of £1,974 183 111 i over last half-year, and a still further increase of £5,327 133 2d over the same period twelve moieties aeo, which reveals a most gratifying state of affairs. You will perceive that after we have made provision for tbe payment of the usual interest on capital invested,ae well as the ordinary allowances tor depreciation on our lands, buildings, and fixtures accounts, we have a divisible net profit of some £8564 193 51 and we propose the Eavmeat of 3s id in £ dividend for this hall-year, educational fund £5. and the balance of £6 16s Li to be carried forward. Some 100 new members have been introduced during the half- year.and the share eapital has &fain ictreased to the ex- tent of £1,904 43 Od. The departmental accounts show suteantial increased support all round; tut your com- mittee are of tho opinion that there is still greater pros- pect for the future development of your Society, and ample room for the extension of our borders, co- operatively speaking, Various departments outside the movement have to receive your support, but every opportunity should be given to the membere to enable them to support their own shops. The vpaira have been heavier than usual this half- year; but it has been absolute necessity which has com- pelled the outlay,and we know that an economic admin- istration, with a keen eye to efficiency, always meets with your approval. One set of ovens at the centre/ premisee, Runcorn, have had to be rebuilt since last bal- ance sheet, and the other two sets are at present under-, going similar treatment. During the lost hag-year we have seen our way to remove the Boot department of Widnes to Victoria-road from Sinue's Cross, to enable the premises to 1:e. more central f,r the whole of the Widnes members ; and up to the present the alterations nave fully justified their existence, which once more gots to prove that our policy should always be " the greatest good for the greatest number." The new bakery, warehouse, and stables at Widnes' are expected to be formally opened ire the course of a few weeks. They are a credit to us,—and our horses are fully enjoying the advantage of their improved conditions, for they have already been placed in the new stables. Our little educational medium—" The Wheatsheaf" is still in great demand, and we would commend the "Oa-operative News" to every intelligent co-operator who wishes to take a lively e3ucatove inte:eißt in the welfare of the co-operative movement. In conclusion, we ask your undivided and loyal support to your own Society, and we undertake, with your pleasure, to administer the affairs of the Socie, y as will merit your fullest and best confidence.—Siened on b-ealf of the Committee, W. THEFT OF MONEY BY AN ALTRINCHAII MAN. At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Friday before the Mayor (presiding), Alderman Roberts and Mr. Samuel Wright, Horan Edwin Nall, a well dressed young man who resided at I,Regent-street, Altrincham, WU charged wi h stealing a purse containing £1 119.11 d. the property of Charles Cooper of 104, Wilderspeol-road. —Defendant who was represented ty Mr. Leonard Harris, of Altrincham, pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable said Mrs. Cooper was cousiu to the defendant and when the latter called at her house on the day in question she naked him in. Mrs. Cooper went upstairs to dress her- self previous to gcing out to pay a rate bill, the money for which was in a puise on a plate in the kitchen down- stairs. Defendant had not been in the house more than a few minutes when he called to Mrs. Cooper to ,-Isay he was going out, and she called to Isiat instructions how to open the door. When Mrs. Cooper got downstairs she found the purse but no money. Information was given to the police and de- fendant was apprehended at Altrincharn and when charged be admitted having gone to Manchester after stealing the money and spent it. Defendant's mother came yesterday (Thursday)to Mrs. Cooper and refunded the money.—Mr. Harris said the defendant bad been left practically in the position of an orphan as his father •died and when he was a few months old his mother married again. He had tried to get employment and had given way to temptation and stolen this money. He suggested as it was the first offence his client ghoull be dealt with under the First Offender's Act.—Mrs. Nall, who said the defendant was her grandchild, stated she bad had him under her care for nineteen years, in fact since his father died. He was twenty years of aye now. His mother had married again 16 years ago. He had been invalided out of the army and had lived with her since that time. He had always behaved well to her.—By the Chief Constable: Defendant ran away and enlisted in the army. Be deserted and got in the han Is of toe police for it. Witness afterwards retracted this statement.—Mrs. Cooper said she did not Want to press the case against him for his mother's sake. —The Bench fined defendant 40s including costs or one month in default, the chairman stating they hoped it would be a warning to him as long as be lived. raosecerriog OF OWNERS OF lISANITARY PROPERTY WARRINGTON.

Ernest Jenkins, a Salvation Army officer, living in Parker-street, Warrington, applied t) the Mayor and Mr. R. Garnett, at the Borough Police CcurE on Tuna. day, for an order exempting him from having his child vaccinated. Re said ha had a conscientious objection to vaccination.
