I don't think you are acting wisely.—Applicant: I am judging from what I have read, and what I have been tol.i. This is my only child.—Mr; Garnett: Is this the feeling 'in the Army generally ?—Appli- cant : I believe it is. Nearly all the officers be- lieve as I do. I am not, however, applying because cners do it. This is a personal applipation.—Mr. ' Garnett: I think it would be worth your while to ob- tain an opinion upon the whole question from a man like Gmeral Booth, because he has got his bead screw(' on rightly if you have not. —Applicant :1 don't know what hisviews are. I have not taken the trouble to aszertain.— Mr. Garnett: If I see him I will speak to him upon the subject. I think he has sense enough and experi- ence enough to lead him to the conclusion that vaccina- tiog, is an important and desirable thing. (To appli- canT) : The time may come when you may have cause to regret what you are now doing.—The Mayor: What better illustration of the value of vaccination could we have than during the smallpox epidemic in Warring- ton. I suppose you are a stranger in the town 2 Applicant: Yes. lam leaving next week. I thought if I decided to have the child vaccinated I would have it done later on. Ido not want it ill just as we are taking it home.—The Mayor: We grant the order very reluctantly. I think you are making a mistalv.

THEFT- BY A WARRINGTON DOMESTIC SERVANT,

- _ At the Warrington Borough P dice Court on Wedner- day morning, before Alderman Sutton (in the chair) and Mr. Geddes, a young girl named Annie Stringer was charged with stealing on Monday. from the dwelling house of Robert John Hough, 369, Liverpeol-road, a silver brooch, a leather purse and Sc. 3d in money, and a feather boa, of the total value of 15s. She pleaded guilty.—The Chief Constable said the prosecuter was a boatman, and the girl had been in his wife's service as general servant for a fortnight or three week.. On Mon- day afternoon Mrs. Hough went out, leaving the girl to take care of the baby. She left 3d. in a drawer upstairs and 9s in another drawer, which was securely locked. The feather boa was in the kitchen. She did not return until half-past five, and on entering the house found that a robbery bad been committed, and that the girl was missing. Two bent pokers were on the floor in the kitchen, and a butcher's steel lay broken on the table. The drawer was broken open, and a portion of the steel was found inside. The cash was missing. Infor- mation was given to the police and inquiries were made which resulted in the girl being apprehended at Moseley She bad then evidently spent the money with the excep- tion of a few coppers. The purse and brooch were found in her possession. The feather boa W 33 found at her •sister's. She said she had only stol-n Se 41. Her whole statement was a series of lies.— Defendant said she asked her mistress if she could co to Moseley. She wanted to visit her sisters. When Mrs. Hongh went out she burst open the drawer and took 21 43. She went on the 'bus to the station and there paid 83. for a half fare to Manchester, and from there paid 51 to go to Mossley. She was going up Stamford- road and saw her brother to whom sho gave threepence She also gave a penny to her stepbrother. She spent the rest of the money. Mrs. Hough put the silver broach in her frock on Sunday. She said she would not give it to her, but get her one with her name on. Mrs. Hough said she would give her 2s a week, but she had not done so.—The girl's father said he was very sorry this had happened. He :had never known the girl to do anything of this sort before. She had a good home, and was well liked. They could not imagine what had induced her te commit the thoft.—The Chief Constable observed that the wakes were being held at Moseley.— Mrs, Hough said the defendant had been a good girl before this. She had been with thin just over a fortnight. She did not ask witness's permission to go to Moseley and never even expressed a wish to co there. She had always fouod the girl truthful.- -The Chairman said the Bench were very disinclined to send the girl to prison, but on the contrary were desir- ous of being as lenient as possible. They would deal with the case unoer the First offenders Act, but it must be understood that a repetition of any, offenc3 of the kind would not be regarded in a similar light. In sash ease it was very certain that the girl would be sent to prison.—Mr. Stringer agreed to pay the costs and be b3und over for tie girl, who was thereupon discharged. A. sitting of this court was hell yesterday at S. Gorge's Hall, before Vice-Chsnce nor Hall.

BLOCKING TEL BACK OF A WAKRINGTON BEERHOIJSE.

Brewery Company and Beerhouse-keeper V. Adjacent Owner.—la this action Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Co, and tteir tenant, Ann Moffat, licensee of the com- pany's beerhouss the British Tar, Winwick-road, War- rington were the plaintiffs, and Jane Taylor. widow, owner of the houses and confectionery works adjoining, was the defendant. Mr. Maberley, with whom was Mr. Lawrence,was for the plaintiff,Mr. Roche and Mr. Coch- ran. being for the defendant. Mr. Maberley said the action was for obstruction of rigiat of way conferred by a deed dated March Ilth, 1851, which gave the owner of the Bcritish Tar a right of way " for tenantable purposes teroueh the passage now in dispute. Much discussiso en Ale' as to the estent of the limitation of the term "tenantable." Mr. Roche said he eneetioned the right of any customer te use the passage Eel a short cot to or from the back door of the public house. If customers were allowed to use the passage it would moan the admission of all Warrington, and the result of the use of the passage by customers of the Brit4sh Tar had been to Cause the defendant a variety of nuisances. Mr. slaberley denied the whole question of nuisance, which. he said, was an afterthought. The plaintlfs had received no complaint of nuisance' by drunken people, and the police bad never complained about toe house. Mr. Maberley called witnesses to prove ,the uuobstriected right of the passage for many years.—Peter Knight, furnacemen, aged 67 years. said he bean to visit ths Brit sh Tar by the back way nearly 50 years ago, in order to fetch beer fI. his grandfather, and ever since, either on his grandparent's behalf or his own behalf, he had neen a a customer for con- sumption off and on the pretaises. VI hen the passage was blocked he remarked : " There will be a bother about this," little dreaming that he would coma to be a witnees in the Chancery Court. (Laughter.) The forge at whmh he worked employed thousands of hands, and fureAce work was very provocative of thirst. (Renewed ' laughter).—Jahn Lee, aged 75 years, formerly a furnace- man, gave evidence to the like effect, stating that he used to visit the British Tar, or, as be called it, the Jolly Tar, thrics a day, and at one period he paid his workmen their wages in the house.—His Honour :—Did you pay for the beer you had on the day you learned you were to become a witness 7—Witness Yes; I never owes nothing for beer: (Laughter.) I never gets nothing like beer given to me. (Renewed laughter.)— His HOLUM! : That is all right.—Thomas °many, coal heaver, related how he had been prevented carrying coal through the passage to the British Tar. He did not know the " gradely " name of the old man who stopped him, but he wore a Scotch cap. (Laughter.)—For the defence. Ralph Norman, nuisance inspector to the Warrington Corporation, said he had called plaintiff Taylor's attention to the state of the yard and its proximity to defendant's sugar house.—Ann Beadehsw, aged 68, daughter of a former owner of de feident's premises, said the passage was, from 1839 to 1857, habitually closed at night.—Jane Taylor, the defendant, said she had been put in a perpetual fear through the acts of some of the custotners [ram the British Tar.—Robert Alexander Tayler, a son of defen- dant. and Albert Finch, lodging with defendant, gave corroborative evidenee.—As Mr. Roche was addressing the Vice. Chancellor on the sufferings of defendant, and her household, his Honour (interposing) said: To a certain extent a public house is always a nuisance to neighbours. From the manner of some of the cuitemers and their conduct in many ways, a public house isalmass necessarily a nuisance to the immediate teighbeurs.— Mr. Cochran pointed out that if the deed had meant to Include customers in the evernent.it woull have specified them.—ln giving judgment, his Honour said the defen- dant's son bad admitted that all went on smoothly be- tween the parties until 1898. Plaintiff Taylor then re- fused to let defendant's carts continue to stand in her yard at a rental of 10s a year, and defeedantfiretaliated by cutting off a proportion of the cult omers to the British Tar, and more or 1993 diverting them to a rival public house. He thought defendant had taken an exaggerated notion of her rights, and this had made her colour the evi,Jence very strongly, as - there was nothing to show that the British Tar was conducted in 1896 or since in any but the reasonable way in which it had been conducted befoze. Being, therefore! ot opinion that the stoppage of customers was an interference with the plaintlf's right of easement far tenantable purposes, he granted an injenc tion in the terms of the petition, and with coats.

THE REPORT.

The directors in ea-Laing t-e;--t-he shareholders the batince sheet, reported that the positiun and prospects of the buoiness contirued to be satisfactory in every way. Including £64,770 18s 41 brought forward Decem- ber 31s, 1898, the net balance of profits,after providing for bad and doubtful debts and rebating current bills at 5 per cent, was £222,952 19s 11d. From that sum the directors recommended a dividend at the rate cf 19 per cent, per annum, free of inconr tax, payable on the Ist August, which amounted to £65,075, a demand at the same rate payable on the Ist November to the share- holders who's names shall be on tue register on October 12th, £65,075. The clactors also recommended to trans- fer to a suspFnse account the unrecovered balance of the notes s;olen on January 23rd, 1899. of £20,610, making a total of £170,760, which left a balance to be carried to next account of £52,192 19s 11d.

THE CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH.—THE STOLEN NOTES. The CHAIRMAN said before proceeding to the purely business consideration of the report be must mention what they would see in the printed report, the fact of the resignation of their colleagues Sir Thomas G. Frost, of Chester. He was sorry to say their colleague bad found the duty of attending their meetings in Len.: don too onerous for a man of his age as he bad been a director for over twenty years. This, of course, was inevitable in the case of such an old bank and so large a direc'orate. He was sure they all witted him many comfortable years in his retirement. (Hear, hear.) There was also another resignation, which perhaps touched them even still more in Warrington. Be referred to that of Mr. Murray. Mr. Murray had been in the service of the bank when it was the old private firm of Pars and Lyon. For many years he was secretary to the bank,and when the bead pffice moved to Loudon, he consented to be- come manager of the branch at Warrington. He thought they could not, sufficiently thank him for his long and faithful services to Parr's Bank. (Hear, bear.) He was pleased that Mr. Murray was still in a measure con- nected with the bank by several treasurership& he bad still kindly consented to bold with various local badies with which he had become identified. Of ceurse they could feel that his successor laboured under some cis- advantage, coming altar so tried and old a friend to Warrington as Mr. Murray. Be was Imre his friends would best please Mr. Murray by taking his successor, Mr. Skinner, into their confidence. Be was sure that Mr. Skinner was well qualified to win their confidence and esteem. (Beat, hear.) Wnen be (the chairman), had the pleasure of meeting them a year befure they had only lately emerged from a long and dreary period ef a 2 per cent. bank rate, but since then the bank rate had never ruled slightly below 3 per cent.; and therefore having been able to employ their money at slightly more remunerative terms, they were able to present once more a fair result, which was on the balance sheet before them. They would observe tbet they had made a separate entry in the report in re- spect of that unfortunate £20,000 of stolen banknotes. They did not consider the loss of these notes came at present under the category of ordinary Dad or doubtful debts. They regarded it rather in the light al a contingent liability. If is eventu- ally did beceme a loss, in whore or in part, he was sure the shareholders would be glad to think that it had been provided tor once and for all. They intended,in every case, where there was a reasonable chance of success, to takesteps to compel the holden toprove their title. Of course, toe reward of £l,OOO was still open to any fortunate gent.emon who could earn it. Amongst the items in the belauce-sheet, the moat noticeable point perhaps was the conziderable augmentation in the money lodged. litters to the enitor.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in al cases send their names and addresei to the Editor. Matters intendtd for publication must be written on one side only of the paper. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

THE SUNDAY TRAFFIC TO FEARNHEAD. Sir,—The Committse of Property 0 xners who arranged and guaranteed an omnibus service of seven days a week on this route., ,have ample reason to be Satisfied with their speculation. During the working days the carriages do a good trade, especially the late ones; and on Sundays they are Po crowded as to leave the im- pression that the bulk of Warrington trippers on those days converge on. Fearahead. All this must be gratify- ing and encouraging to the committee, as it will be sure to make then' popular among some classes, and perhaps aSsist in letting their house.. But, however, this may be the Sunday part of the busineEs is open to objections of the gravest kind. _ .

Christians of all denominations are agreed that on day in every seven should be devoted to zest and wor ship in obedience to the Fourth Commandment. The running of carriages on that day for hire is, therefore, an open and undisguissd vielation of the Sabbath law, and a cruel wrong to the men and animals employed in the traffic. Nay, it is even more than this. It is a cruel wrong to the Sunday trippers themselves inas- much as it diverts their attention altogether from the real objects of the Sabbath, and in numerous cases 'ln- as them for the proper discharge of the duties of the morrow. Another serious objection to the traffic in question is that it is thrust upon us without any reasonable or proper cause. The workieg classes do indeed require fresh air and change of scenery, and I should be the last persen in the world to deny them opportunities for amelioratitg their hard and monotonous life by these good things. But thanks to 'greatly reduced hours of labour, the Saturday half holiaay, and other whole holidays working people have a fair amount of time at their disposal for enjoying the purity and beauty of the country without trespassing upon the day of rest by excursioning of any kind. DILr can it be denied that, however much or little railway directors and carriage owners may desire to please the public their main object in the Sunday traffic is money. And to obtain this they do not hesitate to place the temptations of the Sunday trip and the public-house in the way of the people. Then, as a general rule, the Sunday 'busses may be regarded as feeders for the liquor shops, inasmuon as most of them begin and end their runs at the dcors of the publicans,and thereby promote the interests of "the trade." That the Fearnhead traffic is no exception is proved by the fact that the last carriages on Sunday nights are crowded with men and women whose noisy and unseemly behaviour only too plainly indicates that they have been refreshed by the publican. What a foul blot this singing and laughter is upon our lovely Esg,lish Sabbath night when Heaven and earth seem to woo men to rest and serious thought ! And what a profale and ungrateful return it is to the gracious Being who de- signed the Sabbath to refresh and strengthen us for our toils and responsibilities here, and to prepare us for a better and more enduring rest hereafter ! Just one more objection before I close. I have reason to believe that one or two of the guaranteeing members of the committee ate to be found in Warrington and other pulpits occasionally. Surely in lending their sanction and pecuniary aid to Sunday trafficking these good men must have acted hurriedly.and without due regard to the evils that would !Mew their action. Had they eat down and deliberated upon a plan to frustrate their efforts in the pulpit, and to lead people into the public house, in- stead of into the chapel, they could not have devised a bett-r method than the running of Sunday carriages. As they must by this time know a good deal of the evils which the traffic is inflicting upon a quiet country dis- trict I would hope that they may be lea to do what they can to remedy those evils by removing their cause. Yours truly, 28 July, 1899.

lONA.

ACCIDENT TO Mn. W. PARKES, NEWTON.

On Saturday evening last Mr. W. Parkes of Newton, when leaving a train at Newton-le-Wil- lows Station, thought the train had drawn up to the platform, but a sudden and violent fall on to the ballast proved to him that it had overshot the platform, and he received a bally sprained ankle in consequence. He received medical attention,and is progressing satisfactorily.

LO.O T.HOPE OF EARLESTOWN LODGE.

A public meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall Wargrave-road on Monday preceded by an open air mission led by Bro Woods, L.D. and addressed by Bra's Francis and Dring, Bro Leatherbarraw con- ' tributing the musical portion. Bro Clark pre- sided over a good gathering in the 'Hall, and altar a few remarks cal,ed on Bro Hobbs for a dulcimer solo, Sister Mellor gave a solo, accompanying her- self with the concertina, and sated that it was 25 years that night since she joined the good templars at the other side of the globe an... she had never repented doing so. Bro Wilson gave a mouth organ solc, and Bro Clarke 'introduced Bra Stone- house of Liverpool who in Ws lecture sketched the rise of Cnristianity in Ephesus and the opposition of the silversmiths to the new religion as they feared for their craft which would be endangered by it and showed by quoting passages from speeches delivered at licensed victuallers meetings the similarity between their opposition and that of modern brewers who being in high office in the church seek to stifle the voice of the church not for the sake of religioa but that it would endanger their craft. Bro Plant of Haydock proposed that the thanks of the meet- ing be given to the Lecturer. Mr. Bocock in second- ing, complimented the Lecturer on his able leeture and urged all to try moral suasion in limiting the drink traffic or we cannot get prohibition. Bro Francis in supporting explained that while good templars used moral suasion they would go in for anything that would limit the sale of drink but prohibition was the goal for which they were striving. Bro. Stonehouse having replied the public meeting was doted, and on the lodge being opened Mr. Dring was initiated. Bro. Woods gave notice of convention to be held at Ashton and District Lodge to be held at Tyldesley. the meeting then closed.

HEAVY FINES,

At the Runcorn Police Curt, on Tuesday morning, before Messrs. F. J. Norman and Samuel Taylor, Henry Morris was charged on roman I with stealing a basin and 3 jugs, valued 2i. 61, the property of Anderton & Co.— Mr. F. A. Lake, solicitor, Runcorn, prosecuted. and Morris was defended by Mr. Stringer, solicitor, Sand- bach.—Mr. Lake, outlining the case, said numerous firms were engaged in the manufacture of po-eery for foreign market. The income for the year is £406 14s 81.1, as compered with £334 13s I.ocl for 1896, an increase of £72 Os 101-1. The increase is not abnormal but one which ought to be maintained with the present membership. And we would avail ourselves of this opportunity of asking our eiteemed President, through whom tnese premiums come, to cenvey to the Directors the hearty thanks of the members for such eentribu- tions, and the further evidence of their continued in- terest in the physical and educational well-being of their employes. Although our rsceipts from all sources have been ereater for the year to the 301 a Jane last than any previous year in the history of the lestitute, our ex- penses have not, we are pleasel to state, kept pace with them. This falling off in the number of readers is diffi- cult to explain, unless it is that overtime killed the desire for literature, particularly in the summer months, or is it owing to the increasing popalarity of the evening. parer? Librarian and one of the Auditors Mr. Liversage, to verify the stock. This has ben done, and for sour information we have • pl•asure in euleining an epitome of their report. They say .—" We have taken stock of the books in the Library, and beg to report that according to the Stock I Register, which is practically a duplicate, arsangel in numerical oyder,of the catalogue issued in 1894 aril sup- plementary catalogues of books purchased from that time to the 30 h June lastehereshould be 5123 volumes in the Library. Our verificeti In shows there are only 5.110 volumes, or a deficiency of 13 books. The number of books actually missing (13), represents 1 to every 394 in the Library, a very fair leakage when all the c )ndi- dons ef borrowing are fully considered, but when the number unaccounted for is compared with the number ef volumes issued between November 1894 and June 1899, 4i years, we venture to think the result is very satisfactory, because it shows a loss of only 'O3l per cent., to the turnover during that period, or 1 book missing to every 3,151 issued. We therefore beg to recommend that these 13 volumes 'be replaced, so that the books in circulation will agree with the number in the Stock register. The question of rebinding and repairing has received our very careful attention, and with reference to these two very important branches of library maintenance we found : 363 required rebinding,and 5 requital repairing. There are also 123 volumes which are either exceptionally dirty, worn out, obsolete, or no longer worth replacing by similar volumes, as in the case of scientific works out of date, that we do not recommend their replace- ment, but that authority be given for the sale of the eld books, and the purchase of others to replace, where advisable, and the substitution of currant literature for the obsolete and out of date books." Since the catalogue was issued in 1894, 839 books have been added to the Library, including 181 purchased dur- i nee the year just ended, full particulars of which you will find with the statement of Accounts. The choice of books is, like that of friends, a serious duty, and we are never more c ignisant of that responslbility than when purchasing new volumes, for we remember that a great countryman of ours, Richard de Bury, Bishop of Durham, retieg in praise of books more than five hundred years ago well said "These are the masters who instruct us without rods and ferules, without hard words and anger, without clothes and money ; if you approach them, they are not asleep ; if investigating you interrogate them,they conceal nothing; if you mis- take them they nev grumble; if you are igeorant they cannot laugh at you. The library, therefore, of wisdom is more precioes than all richss and nothing that can be wished for is worthy te be compared with it. Whosoever acknowledges nimself to be a zealous follower of truth of happiness, of wisdom, of science, or even of faith, must of necessity make himself a lover of books." WORKINGMEN'S MISSION, BANK QUAY.

OPENING OF THE NEW SCHOOLS BY COUNCILLOR CROSFIELD,

On Saturday, before a large gathering, Councillor John Crostield, J.P., the donor, opened the new schools, erected in Tnewlii-street, off Liverpool- road, in connection with the Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay.

The buildings consist of one large central room and three classrooms,with cloak room and lavatory accommodation for all the children who will attend the school. The large room will b 3 used for the purposes of the Workingmen's Mission awl Sunday School, and can be divided across the centre during the week when it is in use for day school purposes. Thii room measures 241 t. by 32ft. and has a height of 221 t. 91n. to ceiling. One classroom measures Sift, by 18ft. and two others 181 t. by 151 t. each. Niessrs. Beckett and Co of Warrington and Hart- ford have carried out the contract for the whole work. The buildings are honed with hot water,by Messrs. Killick and C3chran of Liverpool, but have supplementary fireplaces in the classrooms, and the elevations are of Ruabon brick with stone dress- ings. The buildings, we may add, have been erectei from designs and under tha superintendence of Messrs. William and &gar Owen, architects,of War- rington.

The prac-vclings commenced with a procession from the old schools towards the new premises, the members and friends who made up the procession being preceded by the Borough Band. The route ttken was via Liver- pool-road, Green-street, Priestly-street, Aiken-street, Leicester-street, Cartwright-street, Collin-street, Lovely- lane and Powys-street. Includinz the scholars attend- leg the dty schools the number was about 700. Quite a bright appetrance was lent to tho scene by the ban- nerettes of flowers carried by many of the children. The formal ceremony of opening the school was a': once pro- cee led with on arziv AI there.

Mr NANHAN a 'oda, president of the Mission, on be- half of the teachers and wintulttee, handed Coupe llor Crosfisld a silver key, remarking that he hoped tne school woul4 be of service to the residents of Bank Quay acid all concernet

Councillor CR)SFIELD then declared the school open. At a in e'.ling heti a little later in the schools, th 4 R3V Jahn Yonge presided. There were also present, Cl.lll. cillor and Mrs Crosfield, Mr R A and Miss Naylor.Alder- man Roberts,Cou acillors Shaw Green and James Ezans, Mr and Mrs E iward Wallington, Mr Jackson Miss Cowap,M,ssrs Nathan ough,anl T Taylor (super- intendents), T Dale, T AshlPy, and A Tilling (secretary). The singing of a hymn" These walls we to Thy honour raise," commenced the proceedings after the R3V J Yonge had engaged in prayer.

Cduncillor Jim's/ Cnosieen,who wag cordially received, 13,id that he and his wile had mine pleasure in being there that afternoon to witness the ceromeny of opening the workingmea's Mission new Sunday school. (Hear, hear.) It was nearly a quarter of a century since the Sunday school was commeeced and at that time there was not a single school on the west side of the London and North Wes' era Railway. The shoot began with six scholars and now they numbered over five hundred. (Applause.) Ile might venture to say the school had been thrice blessed. It had been a blessing to the child- ren for the instruction they had received ; it had been a blessing to the teachers for the pleasure it had given them in doing something to improve the neigh- bourheed (Elsar, 'hear), and it had also been a bee lug to the parents of the children to see them being brought up in the fear of G el The benefits of a Stand ay school like this were gut beyond computation. (Bear. hear and app'ause ) They could net estimate the benefit that would arise from a school like this, because as those children grow up to be young men and women they would feel the influence they had received while they were being taught there, and it would benefit their ires after ward% (Hear, hear.) He might say in fact, there had been letters written by rule men wh3 had said that they had never forgotten the influence exerted upon them when they were chil Iron in that Sunday school. It was a benefit as she before said, that they could not estimate, and he felt the greate,t sstisfaction possible in knowing that the children of Bank Quay had the opportunity of being cared for and instructed on Sunday in that school. He would repeat to the parents and children how grateful they ought to feel to the teachers who devoted every Sunday in tee year to the instruction of tho children. They got no reward whatever for their services, except the reward ot kn Najaf; that they were doing God's work. (Hear, he a.) The success of the school had been somewhat amazing in the past, end he trusted that the same success would continue in the future. There were two reasons why the school had been so successful. Tre fist was that the Committee had the whole maaage- meat of the school, without anyone interferieg except himself, and he never interfered unless they sent for him. (Hear, bear and -applause.) The second reason was that the school was not connected with any denomi- nation or sect except tee sect called Christians, and he held it had been one of the in,truments of success in the school, because they found children of all denominations attended it. In Stockport, he believed there was a school of that character, and they had no less than three thouFand scholars. He had every hope—in fact he felt confident—teat the success which had attended the school its the past would continue in the future. (Loud applause.) Mr. WILLIAM OWEN, J.P. (the architect), said he re- gretted that the building was not finally completed, so that :they might entirely appreciate Mr. Crosfield's generosity. There were several reasons why tip school was not completed. Fie hop el they would all see the place when it was finished. He thought that when ho teld them that the area of land there which Mr Crosfield had provided was five times as large as the old building and that the area of the scheol was considerably in excess of the old one, they would think Mr Crosfield had done his best for the neighbourhood. (Bear, hear.) Toe building WAS to he used for two purposes—religious education and secular education. He wanted to remind , Mr. Crosfield that he was present when Mr. Crosfield opened a new building which was also for the purpose 1 of education. Mr. Crosfield opened the School of Art in Warrington. They should remember that they would not have had that building had it not been for his instrumentality. It showed that Mr. Crosfield had at heart the religious and senular education of Warrington. He (Mr. Owen) thought that if anyone had covered the ground of religious wad secular education in Warrington and endeavoured to do his best for their good,it was Mr. Crosfield (Applause). In conclusion he hoped the schoo would answer its purpose. He had tried to design it so that it should be light and airy in summer, and light and warm in winter—(applause)—Eo that in winter and summer it would serve its purpose. No one would be more pleased than he—except Mr. Crosfied—if the scheol proved suocessful to those who used it. (Applause.) After the singing of another hymn, Alderman ROBERTS spoke. It gave him very much pleasure to be present on the occasion of the opening of those schools. He was present on the day when the foundation stone was laid, an I he then lead an oppor- tunity of saying a few words which he thought he could not very well amplify one the present occasion, but as he was down on the programme to occupy ten minutes he would again address them. He was glad to be there as he considered the idea of that occasion a noble one. The building had been excellently designed by the architect, and admirably carried out by the builders. He did not think he was exaggerating when he said it was superior to any place of a similar class in Warring- ton. He hoped that the purpose for which it had been given wou'd be by its results gratifying to all connected with the school. There was one good circumstance con- nected with the use of the place as a day school, and that was that it was undenominetional and unsectarian. The building would be an addition to his old school, People's College. One thing that made it more gratify- ing to him was that an old school fellow of his, Mr. Edward Wallington, was now headmaster. (Applause.) Having said so much with regard to the secular side, he would now say a word or two with reference to the re- ligious side. They did not fully comprehend at the pre- sent day the enormous value of religious education given in the Sunday schools of the United Kingdom. It was the primary duty of all parents to teach religion to their own children. In this country the parents did not fulfil their duties as they ought to do ; and in many cases, foster parents had to carry out the obligations of the parents. The Sunday School acted the part of the foster parent sup- plemented by the teachers who ;did their work without any pay or reward of the character they were accus- tomed to look for in the ordinary sense. There were times when teachers felt much discouraged, especially in the hot w eather in summer time with cramped rooms It was a difficulty, but teachers had their reward,some. times in this world, but to a great extent they had to wait for the world which was to come. When Sunday School teachers were downcast and discouraged because

BETTER DISOIPLINS AID A LITTLE WEEDING- OUT REQUIRED.

At a meet'ag fof the Council on Tuesday evening the mihutes of the Fire Engine Committee came up for con- tirnwioa. They contained a resolution to the effect that the resignations of the members of the brigade with thA exception of the Superintendent and Deputy Super- intendent be accepted, and that the Superintendent con- fer with the Borough Surveyor, and that they be in- structed to tske such steps as they may deem necessary ta secure the services of men to take the place of the mAmbers resigned.

C3uncillor PunaProw moved the adoption of the minutes. Witn !Wei erica t) the fire at the works or Messrs. B trker and Sms, tydock-street, he said the men workad well and they had received a letter compli- meeting them oa their work. With regard to the sale of waft old engine to the Penketh Tanning Company. for £l5, they had another engine of rather an ancient type wilich they could use in case of necessity. The mos important resolution on the minutes was that relatinet to the resignation of the firemen. There had been somg difficulty for some time past, he said, between the brigade and the committee, but he thought it mighe have been settled without tt e correspondence there hat ben. Serge int Monks had written to the Town Clerd and the Chairman of the committee, and wanted an ink terview. Toe Town Clerk wrat ein reply to grant one. They then received the letter pub lidhed on the agenda piper, giving notice of resignation of the firemen, and a deputation was not sent to meet them. The Committee were very indignant at the letter, and were unanimous in acc Toting the resignation, and refusing to go down to the Fire Engine Station as the men requested—as he should say—demanded. Since then instruc'ions had been given to Superintendent Turner to form a new hrigaite. At tho time the present brigade expired they would be prepared wi tn a new one. It was said alter wardi that the fire brigade did not wish to resign but to come to terms, tut that oug ht to have come from Ser- geant Monks who began the correvondence.

Toe MAYOR suggested if there were any signs of peace, let there be peace. Councillor PLIMPTON replied they were making for peace, but although they had a goad fira brigade, he felt the-e were some members who would do with weeding our. Before they couA come to any terms they must have a letter from Sergeant Monks asking for those terms. Until that wag done he could not see they could do so. `rue brigade required that if a fire broke out in the country, all the firemen who turned out should be allowed to remain until the fire was extinguished. The Committee held that was impracticable, because if there was a fire in the town they would be in a difficulty. The Committee thought it was unreasonable the men should ask it. They had a high opinion of the brigade, but the Committee must hold their position, and the brigade theirs. Councillor ROGERS seconded the adoption of the minutes.

Councillor JOHN ROBERTS asked if a fire broke out, what position would they bs in ? A new brigade was not to be drilled in five minutes. He thought it was an important matter as far as owner 3 of property were concerned.

Alderman FIIITCHINSON thought the acion of the com- mittee was reasonable and right. He had heard that the men had appproaohed thesuperinte ndent,and wished to say to the committee that they were sorry for what they had done.; that it was not business like, and they wished to rectify it, but they were informed it was too late. He pointed out that men could not be got together in a few days, and that if they showed their error, the committee should try and rectify matters. Councillor TINNION said he was not present at the committee meeting, but he fully approved of what they had done. The discipline of the brigad was at a low ebb. There had been slight causes ot difference between the committee and the brigade. They had found th'ngs did not run smoothly, and in the whole of the brigade generally there was a want of discipline and organisa- tion. They had endeavoured to rectify some things and Hi had resulted in an open rupture. It had been growing for some time, and that was the position they found themselves in now. They bad a splendid lot of material and a good lot of firemen. Some had been in the bri- gade several years, and perhaps had been firemen long enough. He was of the opinion that nothing but good would Come of it. They would get a brigade composed of old material but on a better footing. He ct.uld not see why they should not have in the brigade a number of the polices It was desirable in re•organising to have something like that. . _ _

• Councillor EVANS asked what was the Custom in other towns when men were called out to a fire. The deputy- chairman had said the committee had been acquainted with the fact of these differences for some time. What had been done to remedy those things ? Alderman SUTTON said he took it for granted that in taking that action the committee were satisfied, and wore very careful how they dealt with the brigade. If men of ability had to be replaced, it would have to be by those who were not so folly skilled. They should take into consideration the importance of the position of these men, and not to have unexperienced men to deal with firet.

Councillor HEATON thought they might leave it to the committee. Be believed they would do what was rig! t. Be w,uld like to remind the committee it bad taken a number of t etre to get men near the fire station. At pr sent there were 18 living within 300 yards, and at one time there were only four. They would remember it had been suggested to the Council they should get houses near the fire station. He thought perhaps both sides had not been in a very good humour. Councillor PLIIMPTON in reply to Councillor John Roberts, said he need not have any fear if a fire broke out. They would be prepared for any emergency. Thsy had the good of the town at heart,and they could mike preparations. There were a number cf man in their own employ,and a brigade could very soon be prepared. In reply to Alderman Hutchinson, they were quite pre- pared to do what was right. If Sergeant Monks would condescend to write to the committee they would, no doubt, be pleased to receive them. It would take some little time to answer the inquiries of Councillor Evans. The men had not carried out their instructions. An an in- stance he might mention that they had made a resolu- tion th+t a m -Al should remain at the fire station not more than three hours after the engine had gone. The committee thought that was long enough for the brigadei to know whether they wanted anything else in any shape or form and tcrsend back for it if they required it. There had been a fire recently in Arnley meadows,which had cost the ratepayers £l3 or £l4. The stack was burning for two or three days. There was a man,named Ratcliffe, left at the station. He should have gone at the end of three hours but be stayed there eleven, and charged 174 81 The committee refused to pay it, acid only paid 5s ed. That was the commencement of the bother. Another resolution was that every man should eater the time of his arrival at the station on a slate, but the men refused to comply with it. The minutes were then passed.

THE GIRL ANTROBUS.

A letter was read from the Cheshire County Asylum. Parkside, Macclesfield, stating that Margaret Antrobas (an inmate of the Runcorn Union Workhouse, who had been commi:ted at Runcorn Petty Sessions fir 14 days, for wilful damage to glass, on the 30,13 of June), had been admitted to that institution as a pauper lunatic. Norman laved that publicity would be given to the fact, as it appeared the girl conducted her- self in a similar way to that in which she had dose in Runcorn. The Chairman concurred.

RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE.-CHESHIRE GIUND DIVISION. - - -

Trio half-yearly meeting of the above" " was held on Saturday in the Primitive Methodist School Room. Winsford. There was a good attendance isf officers and representatives from Crewe, Winsford, Wes- ton. Runcorn, and Northwich.—The meeting was pre- sided over by Mr. T. Cooke, G.W.P., of Runcorn, who made a few encouraging remarks about the position of the order, and reminded the members that this was the Jubilee year of the society. Be announced that there would be a demonstration in Runcorn early in August. —The various officers gave their reports which were deemed satisfactory and accepted.—Mr. 'roomer; J. Holt (G. Scribe) in an exhaustive report, dealt with the financial and numerical position of the society. fie stated that there were now 505 adult members and 218 elders, an increase of 34 on the half-year. It was de- cided that the annual meting would be held at Run- corn, in January, 1900. The Grand Division was then closed.

WARRINGTON CORPORA- TION BILL. SUMMARY OF PROVISIONS • This Bid has passed both Houses of Parliament, and now only awaits the Royal Assent, vehicle will be given in due course.—The Act will come into force on the day on which it receives the Royal Assent, and as it con- tains many new powers and regulations, it behoves every inhabitant of the Borough old enough to under- stand them, to make himself acquainted with the provisions of the Act. Besides the Schedules to the Act, there are 179 Sections divided into 20 parts dealing with the fo lowing matters :—(1) Definitions, (2) Water, (3) Street widenings, &c., (4) New Streets), (5) New Build- ings, (6) Dangerous Structures and Obstructions, (7) Sanitory Provisions, (8) Infectious Disease, (9) Tuber- culosis, (10) Common Lodging Houses, (11) Gas, (12) Street Advertisements. (13) Purchase of Lands. (14) Recreation Grounds (15) Hackney Carriages, (16) Pollee Provisions, (17) Rates, &c., (18) Thrift Fund, (19), Financial Provisions, (20) Miscellaneous. The Waterworks part of the Act has been altered con- [ siderably since it was first introduced into Parliament. The whole of the works at Winwick including the pumping station, tunnels and service reservoir have been sanctioned and the high level service reservo r at Appletou has been allowed. The proposed collecsting reservoir on Daresbury Brook was struck out or the Bill by the Committee of the Commons and the pro- posed reservoir at Dennow was withdrawn by the Cor- poration in the Committee of the Lerds. We, however, understand that arrangements have been made by which both Deresbury and Dennow reservoirs may be made at some future date. The Corporation have obtained power to make bye- laws with regard to the pipes and all other water appli- ances. They may require every house to be suppled by a separate pipe and a consumer ,of water will be liable to payment of rates for a period of six months after he has ceased to occupy the premises supplied un- less he give notice in writing that he has ceased to occupy the premises. The Corporation have obtained power to supply fit- tings and to put up public drinking fountains. They I may also purchase for the protection of their works in adaition to the lands scheduled 140 acres. Power is given to widen Bridge-street and Sankey-et. from Rylandseitreet to Market Gate and from there to Trinity Chnrch and the principle of betterment is cott- ceded in that any benefit accruing by reason of the improve:nent of any street shall be set off against the payment of compensation. The Corporation are authorised to esquire compul- pulsorily land for the extension of the Hospital and Sani- tary Depot. The agreement with the London and North-Western Railway Company as to the widening of tee new brig over Slutcher's-lane is confirmed, ana the money agreed to be paid is authorised to be borrowed. Several very important Sections are contained in the Act with regard to New Streets and Buildings. All placs must in future be submitted on tracing cloth, and will be void unless acted upon within twe years after depcsit. No new street will be allowed more than 120 yards in length without an intersecting strest. The coninuation of existing street; will be deemed new streets, and shall not be less than 40 feet in width. No buildings will be allowed until the street is farmed, and , the Corporation may vary the position and direction of all new streets. They may also declare where any new street begins and ends. All waste lands in the borough and all gardens and forecourts are to be fenced. No new building shall exceed in height the width of the street or any adjoining building. ' The height of all new chimneys Is regulated according to the horse-power of any engine used in connection therewith. Power is given to require all yards and open spaces to be paved. The entrances to courts and yards may not be closed and no building may be entered or converted without the sanction of tbe Corporation. The provisions of the TOVVIIs Improvement Clauses Act with regard to dangerous buildings may be extended to any structure which the Corporation may require to be removed. Trees and shrubs overhanging streets may be re- moved and sky signs are prohibited. Catheriels and and other like projectione mar be removed. The Corporation mty appotut any numoer of Inspec- tors of Nuisances with full powers. They may require houses to be drained by combined operation and where separate sewers are provided they may require separate drains for sewage and surface water respectively. Penalties are provided for damage to drains and fell power is given for their inspection The Corporation may make Bye-laws with regard to the cleansing of cisterns and no dwelling-house can in future be occupied until an efficient supply of water is provided. The Corporation may require urinals •to be provided for every inn, public house or place of public entertain- ment, and may require all exisiting ones to be removed or altered. They may also themselves construct public conveniences-in or under any street and they may charge for the removal of trade refuse. The provisions of the Act with regard to infectious diseases are most important. Persons washing or mangl- ing clothes, cewkeepers, dairymen, mllksellers, anti principals of schools are required on demand to furnish lists of their customers or scholars. No person may ' carry on business connected with food while he is suffer- ing from an infectious diseases or lives in a house in which any such disease exists. Wakes over dead 1 bodies are prohibited and bed and body clothing may I be required to be purified. Dairymen and milksellers I are to notify infectious disease among their servants. No book shall be returned to the Harem which has been exposed to infection. The Corporation may provide and charge for the services of nurses and may make bye-lews tor regulating the conduct of patients in the hospitals and preventing persons from entering the hospitals or the grounds thereof. Very stringent regulations are made with regard to the sale of milk. Persons selling milk from a cow suffer- ing from tuberculosis are liable to a penalty of £lO. Notice of the diseases must be given and all diseased cows must be isolated. Power is given to take samples of milk and to inspect cows whether within or without the borough if milk from such cows is sold within the borough. All lodging-houses must be provided with proper sani- tary conveniences. The Corporation may make bye-laws with regard to gas pipes and fittings. No hoarding or billposting station can be hereafter erected without the sanction of the Corporation. The Act authorises the Corporation to purchase the land required for the new police buildings, Technical Institute, extension of hospitals, extension of sanitary depot, street widening and improvements, river:wharves land Victoria Park, and also St. James's old burial [ ground. They are authorised to erect pavidons and buildings in any park or recreation ground, and let the same. They are also empowered to provide a place for games, and to set apart any part of any recreation ground for their use. They may pay for public bands, and charge for admission to any recreation ground, and may provide seats and charge for the use thereof. Inspectors appointed by the Corporation, may stop nd examine all public.vehicles, to see, that the lawsand bye-laws are duly observed. Netice of circus and other processions through the streets' must be given under a penalty of £5 The Act gives power to terminate the offices of Assis- tant Overseer and Collector of Porr Ratet, and the duties of those offices may be discharged by officers of the Corporation. All rates may be demanded in one demand note, and one set of books may be prescribed. The Corporation may establish a Thrift Fund, on a scheme to be approved by the Secretary of State. The Act authorises the Corporation to borrow the followitsg sums of money For Waterworks purposes £147,000 „ New Police Premises „ Purchase of Victoria Park lO,OOO „ Extensions of hospitals & sanitary der o•, 14,400 „ Erection of river wharves „ Technical Schools 15,000 „ The widening of Slutcher's-lane... 2,000 „ The widening of Bridge-street and other improvements 50,000 50 years is allowed for the repayment ol the meney. The whole of the Corporation debt is consolidated and the periods of repayment equated. New properties may be inserted in the rate book dining the currency of any rate and compositions to owners will not be allowed after a date fixed by the Corporation. Power is. given to close the Baths and to charge for the exclusive use thereof. All articles left in the zarkets are forfeited and may be sold. The Corporation may take posse3sion of any stall or stand in the marketer fair for non-payment of rent. All undertakings by owners are to be binding on their successors. Power is given to any constable or member of the Fire Brigade to break open any premises in case of fire

LATEST NE S. TORNADO IN AMERICA. A SEAPORT DESTROYED. New YORK, Turrestal• A series of devastating storms took place yesterday le various parts of the country. Half a millson acres were affected in the Mississippi Valley, and the crops in that area are said to have been destroyed. The tewa Cl Carrabella, a prosperous seaport in Florida, has been practically wiped out by the tornado. Many vessels well driven asuore and wrecked. There have been Boole fatalities, but the loss of life is not yet accurateli known. At St. Marks in Florida, where the storm was also experienced in all its severity, fifteen persons were drowned. A RECORD IN CHILD-BIRTH. A record case of childbirth occurred at Bletchley, near Fenny, Stratford, yesterday where a widow gave birth in a lodging house, to six children. Dr. Nicholson at- tended. They were five months' children and were all still-born. Dr. Nicholson embalmed the bodies,and selot them to the Anatomical Museum, London. The poor woman was subsequently removed to the Union 10" firrnary. at Fenny, Stratford. THE MURDER OF THE DOMINICAN P RESIDALNT. EXECUTION OF TEE ASSASSINS. NEW YORK, THURSDAY MORNINE. A despatch from San Domingo to the "New York Journal' announces that two of the assassins of the late President Heureux have been captured. They were promptly shot. The country is now quiet. A HUNGRY CHESHIRE FOX. The ravages of foxes in Cheshire are causing serioUs losses to farmers. Yesterday morning, Mr. Crewe, or The Grange Farm, Wybuebury. near Crewe, repeats the loss of no fewer than 60 chickens, many of which were well bred, Reynard carried off 20 of the chickene and lefttlas remainder in the orcaard after having bitter' off their heads. There are several litters of cubs in the neighbourhood. LATEST LOCAL WEATHER FORECAST. VIOLENT PRISONERS AT RUNCORN. At the Runcorn' Police Court, on Saturday, before Messrs. F. J. and A. R. Norman, Edward Morris Ann Morris, husband and wife, were charged with being drunk and disorderly in High street on the previous daY. Sergeant Cooper stated that his attention was called to , the prisoners in High-street near the Holyhead Harhnur• They were both drunk and using bid language. Willi the assistance--of P.O. Nicholls he brought them to tbe pol,ce station. —P.C. Nichoils corroborated.—Cbarles Duttort,of the London and North Wcstern Railway Betel stated that the two prisoners came into his house and asked for a pint of beer. He refused to serve them 011, account of their condition, and they made use of Ma language. The male prisoner took up a stoolsand dashed it on the counter in a threatening manner. He gave le" form,tion to the police.—Sergeant Gill spoke to the now duct of the prisoners while in the cells. Ile had to keel) watch to prevent them doing any damage. They wig!, the most violent prisoners they had bad in the celi' during the past five years.—The female prisoner Was 4 fined ss. and 9s. costs, or seven days hard labour, enu,, the man was committed to gaol for 21 days' her' labour.
